N_{D}=\overline{\otimes}_{w\in D}N_{w}
, using the fact that
M_{\Sigma_{i}}
is strongly prime and
[
Is20
, Lemma 4.1]
, we find
w\in D
such that
M_{\Sigma_{i}}\prec_{N_{D}^{s}}N_{w}^{s}
. Thus,
M_{\Sigma_{i}}\prec_{Q}N_{w}^{t}
, contradicting condition (3) from the hypothesis since
|\Sigma_{i}|\geq 2
, and
\Sigma=\Sigma_{i}\cup\Sigma_{i}^{\perp}
and thus
N_{w}^{t}\subset Q=M_{\Sigma_{i}\cup\Sigma_{i}^{\perp}}
. This proves (
9.3
).

We continue by treating separately two cases.

Case 1.

C\not=\emptyset
.

If
v^{\prime}\in C
, then
\text{st}(v)\subset\Sigma\subset\text{st}(v^{\prime})
. As
v
is untransvectable, we get
v^{\prime}=v
and thus
C=\{v\}
.

Since
M_{v}\subset Q=N_{\Delta}^{t}=(N_{\Delta_{j}\cup\Delta_{j}^{\perp}})^{t}
and
|\Delta_{j}|\geq 2
, condition (2) from the hypothesis implies that
N_{\Delta_{j}}^{t}\nprec_{Q}M_{v}
, for every
1\leq j\leq n
.
Using this fact, that
Q=M_{v}\overline{\otimes}(\overline{\otimes}_{i=1}^{m}M_{\Sigma_{i}})=N_{D}^{t}\overline{\otimes}(\overline{\otimes}_{j=1}^{n}N_{\Delta_{j}})
and claim (
9.3
), Lemma

5.3
implies that we can find a decomposition
Q=N_{D}^{s}\overline{\otimes}(\overline{\otimes}_{j=1}^{n}N_{\Delta_{j}})^{t/s}
, for some
s>0
, and a unitary
u\in Q
such that
uM_{v}u^{*}=N_{D}^{s}
. In particular,
N_{D}^{t}\prec_{Q}M_{v}
. Since
M_{v}\subset Q=N_{D\cup D^{\perp}}^{t}
, condition (2) from the hypothesis implies that
|D|=1
. Thus,
D=\{w\}
, for some
w\in\Lambda
, which implies that
M_{v}\prec_{P}N_{w}^{t}
. This finishes the proof in Case 1.

Case 2.

C=\emptyset
.

Then
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\Sigma_{m}
and
we may assume that
v\in\Sigma_{1}
. We claim that
v
is untransvectable in
\Sigma_{1}
. Indeed, assume that
\text{lk}(v)\cap\Sigma_{1}\subset\text{st}(v^{\prime})\cap\Sigma_{1}
, for
v^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{1}
.
Since
\text{st}(v)\subset\Sigma
, we have that
\text{lk}(v)=(\text{lk}(v)\cap\Sigma_{1})\cup(\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{1})
. Since we also have
\text{st}(v^{\prime})\supset(\text{st}(v^{\prime})\cap\Sigma_{1})\cup(\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{1})
, it follows that
\text{lk}(v)\subset\text{st}(v^{\prime})
. Since
v
is untransvectable in
\Gamma
, we get
v^{\prime}=v
, which proves our claim.

We now claim that
D=\emptyset
. Indeed,
Q=N_{D}^{t}\overline{\otimes}(\overline{\otimes}_{j=1}^{n}N_{\Delta_{j}})
, so Theorem
I
(1) ensures that there exist
s>0
and
I\subset\{1,\dots,m\}
such that
N_{D}^{s}
is unitarily conjugate to
\overline{\otimes}_{i\in I}M_{\Sigma_{i}}
inside
Q
. In particular,
M_{\Sigma_{i}}\prec_{Q}N_{D}^{s}
, for every
i\in I
, which contradicts (
9.3
). This contradiction proves that
D=\emptyset
.

Since
M_{\Sigma_{1}}
is strongly prime and
Q=M_{\Sigma_{1}}\overline{\otimes}M_{\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{1}}=(\overline{\otimes}_{j=1}^{n}N_{\Delta_{j}})^{t}
, we deduce that
M_{\Sigma_{1}}\prec_{Q}N_{\Delta_{j}}^{t}
, for some
1\leq j\leq n
(see
[
Is20
, Lemma 4.1]
).

Since
N_{\Delta_{j}}
is prime, by applying Lemma
2.8
we can find a decomposition
Q=N_{\Delta_{j}}^{s}\overline{\otimes}N_{\Delta\setminus\Delta_{j}}^{t/s}
, for some
s>0
, and a unitary
\eta\in Q
such that
\eta M_{\Sigma_{1}}\eta^{*}=N_{\Delta_{j}}^{s}
.
Therefore, we have a
*
-isomorphism
\theta_{0}:M_{\Sigma_{1}}\rightarrow N_{\Delta_{j}}^{s}
given by
\theta_{0}(x)=\eta x\eta^{*}
.
Since
\Sigma_{1}\subsetneq\Sigma\subset\Gamma
, we get
|\Sigma_{1}|\leq|\Gamma|-1=d
.

We now observe that conditions (2) and (3) from the hypothesis are inherited by
\theta_{0}
from
\theta
. To see that
\theta_{0}
satisfies condition (2), assume by contradiction that there is a full subgraph
F\subset\Delta_{j}
such that
|F|\geq 2
,
N_{F}^{s}\prec_{N_{\Delta_{j}}^{s}}\theta_{0}(M_{v})
and
\theta_{0}(M_{v})\prec_{N_{\Delta_{j}}^{s}}(N_{F\cup(F^{\perp}\cap\Delta_{j})})^{s}
.
Since
F\cup(F^{\perp}\cap\Delta_{j})\subset F\cup F^{\perp}
and
\theta_{0}(x)=\eta x\eta^{*}
for
x\in M_{\Sigma_{1}}
, we get that
N_{F}^{t}\prec_{L}M_{v}
and
M_{v}\prec_{L}(N_{F\cup F^{\perp}})^{t}
, which contradicts condition (2) for
\theta
. Similarly,
\theta_{0}
also satisfies condition (3).

Since
v
is untransvectable in
\Sigma_{1}
, we can apply the induction hypothesis to
\theta_{0}
to find a vertex
w\in\Delta_{j}
such that
\theta_{0}(M_{v})\prec_{N_{\Delta_{j}}}N_{w}^{s}
. We have
M_{v}\prec_{Q}N_{w}^{t}
, which finishes the proof of Case 2.
∎

10.
From strong intertwining to stable isomorphism

Assume the setting of Notation
6.1
. Theorems
7.1
,
8.1
and
9.1
provide instances when there exists a graph isomorphism
\alpha:\Gamma\rightarrow\Lambda
such that
M_{v}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{\alpha(v)}
and
N_{\alpha(v)}\prec_{P}^{s}M_{v}
, for every
v\in\Gamma
. The following result clarifies the relationship between the algebras
M_{v}
and
N_{\alpha(v)}
entailed by the last condition.

Lemma 10.1
.

In the setting from Notation
6.1
, assume that
v\in\Gamma
and
w\in\Lambda
are such that
M_{v}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{w}
and
N_{w}\prec_{P}^{s}M_{v}
. Then the following hold:

(1)

Assume that
M_{\emph{st}(v)}
,
N_{\emph{st}(w)}
are II
1
factors, and the projection
p\in P
from Notation
6.1
belongs to
\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\otimes N_{\emph{st}(w)}
. Then there exist a unitary
u\in pPp
and a decomposition
p(\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\otimes N_{\emph{st}(w)})p=N_{w}^{s}\overline{\otimes}N_{\emph{lk}(w)}^{t}
, for
s,t>0
, such that
uM_{\emph{st}(v)}u^{*}=p(\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\otimes N_{\emph{st}(w)})p
and
uM_{v}u^{*}=N_{w}^{s}
. In particular,
M_{v}
and
N_{w}
are stably isomorphic II
1
factors.

(2)

In general, we can find nonzero projections

p\in M_{v},q\in N_{w}
and a
*
-homomorphism
\psi:qN_{w}q\rightarrow pM_{v}p
such that
pM_{v}p\prec_{pM_{v}p}\psi(qN_{w}q)
. Moreover, there exist nonzero projections
s\in M_{v}
and
t\in N_{w}
and a unital embedding
tN_{w}t\subset sM_{v}s
which has finite index.

Proof.

(1) Since
M_{v}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{w}
and
N_{w}\prec_{P}^{s}M_{v}
, Proposition
3.9
implies that
M_{\text{st}(v)}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{\text{st}(w)}
and
N_{\text{st}(w)}\prec_{P}^{s}M_{\text{st}(v)}
.
Since
M_{\text{st}(v)},N_{\text{st}(w)}
are II
1
factors and
\text{st}(v)^{\perp}=\text{st}(w)^{\perp}=\emptyset
, Lemma
2.16
(2) implies that
M_{\text{st}(v)}^{\prime}\cap pPp=\mathbb{C}p
and
N_{\text{st}(w)}^{\prime}\cap qPq=\mathbb{C}q
.
These facts together with Proposition
2.4
, (2) and (3), imply that
M_{\text{st}(v)}\nprec_{P}N_{\Lambda_{0}}
and
N_{\text{st}(w)}\nprec_{P}M_{\Gamma_{0}}
, for every full subgraphs
\Gamma_{0}\subsetneq\text{st}(v)
and
\Lambda_{0}\subsetneq\text{st}(w)
. By using again that
\text{st}(v)^{\perp}=\text{st}(w)^{\perp}=\emptyset
, and
M_{\text{st}(v)}
,
N_{\text{st}(w)}
are II
1
factors, Theorem
3.14
gives unitaries
\xi,\eta\in pPp
such that

\text{Ad}(\xi)(M_{\text{st}(v)})\subset p(\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\otimes N_{\text{st}(w)})p
and
\text{Ad}(\eta)(p(\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\otimes N_{\text{st}(w)})p)\subset M_{\text{st}(v)}
.

Thus,
\text{Ad}(\eta\xi)(M_{\text{st}(v)})\subset\text{Ad}(\eta)(p(\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\otimes N_{\text{st}(w)})p)\subset M_{\text{st}(v)}
. Proposition
3.9
(1) gives
\eta\xi\in M_{\text{st}(v)}
, hence
\text{Ad}(\eta\xi)(M_{\text{st}(v)})=M_{\text{st}(v)}
. This implies that
\text{Ad}(\xi)(M_{\text{st}(v)})=p(\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\otimes N_{\text{st}(w)})p
.

Since
M_{v}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{w}
and
N_{w}\prec_{P}^{s}M_{v}
, Lemma
2.18
implies that
\text{Ad}(\xi)(M_{v})\prec^{s}_{\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\otimes N_{\text{st}(w)}}N_{w}
and
N_{w}\prec^{s}_{\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\otimes N_{\text{st}(w)}}\text{Ad}(\xi)(M_{v})
.
Since we have that
M_{\text{st}(v)}=M_{v}\overline{\otimes}M_{\text{lk}(v)}
,
N_{\text{st}(w)}=N_{w}\overline{\otimes}N_{\text{lk}(w)}
, and
M_{v},M_{\text{lk}(v)},N_{w},N_{\text{lk}(w)}
are II
1
factors, the conclusion follows from Lemma
2.8
.

(2) Since
N_{w}\prec_{P}M_{v}
, we can find projections
r\in N_{w},q\in M_{v}
a nonzero partial isometry
\zeta_{2}\in qPr

and a
*
-homomorphism
\theta_{2}:rN_{w}r\rightarrow qM_{v}q
such that
\theta_{2}(y)\zeta_{2}=\zeta_{2}y
, for every
y\in rN_{w}r
.
Since
\zeta_{2}^{*}\zeta_{2}\in(rN_{w}r)^{\prime}\cap rN_{\Lambda}r=(N_{w}^{\prime}\cap N_{\Lambda})r
, we can write
\zeta_{2}^{*}\zeta_{2}=rr^{\prime}
, for a projection
r^{\prime}\in N_{w}^{\prime}\cap N_{\Lambda}
.

We claim that
M_{v}\prec_{P}rN_{w}rr^{\prime}
. Otherwise, if
M_{v}\nprec_{P}rN_{w}rr^{\prime}=(rr^{\prime})(N_{w}r^{\prime})(rr^{\prime})
, Remark
2.2
(6) would imply that
M_{v}\nprec_{P}N_{w}s
, where
s\in\mathcal{Z}(N_{w}r^{\prime})=\mathcal{Z}(N_{w})r^{\prime}
is the central support of
rr^{\prime}
. On the other hand, since
M_{v}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{w}
,
N_{w}\prec_{P}^{s}M_{v}
, and
s\in N_{w}^{\prime}\cap N_{\Lambda}
, Lemma
6.6
gives that
M_{v}\prec_{P}N_{w}s
. This gives a contradiction, and finishes the proof of the claim.

Next, since
M_{v}\prec_{P}rN_{w}rr^{\prime}
, by Proposition
2.5
(1) we can find projections
e\in M_{v},f\in rN_{w}r
, a partial isometry
\zeta_{1}\in fr^{\prime}Pe
and a
*
-homomorphism
\theta_{1}:eM_{v}e\rightarrow fN_{w}f
such that
\theta_{1}(x)\zeta_{1}=\zeta_{1}x
, for every
x\in eM_{v}e
.

Let
q_{0}=\theta_{2}(f)=\theta_{2}(\theta_{1}(e))
,

\rho:eM_{v}e\rightarrow q_{0}M_{v}q_{0}
the
*
-homomorphism given by
\rho(x)=\theta_{2}(\theta_{1}(x))
and
\zeta=\zeta_{2}\zeta_{1}\in qPe
. Then
\rho(x)\zeta=\zeta x
, for every
x\in eM_{v}e
.
Since
\zeta_{2}^{*}\zeta_{2}=rr^{\prime}
and
\zeta_{1}\in rr^{\prime}P
is nonzero we deduce that
\zeta^{*}\zeta=\zeta_{1}^{*}(\zeta_{2}^{*}\zeta_{2})\zeta_{1}=\zeta_{1}^{*}\zeta_{1}\not=0
, and therefore
\zeta\not=0
.
Moreover,
q_{0}\zeta=\rho(e)\zeta=\zeta e=\zeta
, hence
\zeta\in q_{0}Pe
.
Since
\zeta(eM_{v}e)\subset(q_{0}M_{v}q_{0})\zeta
, by Proposition
3.9
(1) we also deduce that
\zeta\in M_{\text{st}(v)}
and thus
\zeta\in q_{0}M_{\text{st}(v)}e=q_{0}M_{v}e\overline{\otimes}M_{\text{lk}(v)}
.

Note that for every
\delta\in M_{\text{lk}(v)}
we have
\rho(x)\text{E}_{M_{v}}(\zeta(1\otimes\delta))=\text{E}_{M_{v}}(\zeta(1\otimes\delta))x
, for every
x\in eM_{v}e.

Consequently, we can find a nonzero
\gamma\in q_{0}M_{v}e
such that
\rho(x)\gamma=\gamma x
, for every
x\in eM_{v}e
. This in particular implies that
q_{0}M_{v}q_{0}\prec_{q_{0}M_{v}q_{0}}\rho(eM_{v}e)
. Since
\rho(eM_{v}e)\subset\theta_{2}(fN_{w}f)
, we deduce that
q_{0}M_{v}q_{0}\prec_{q_{0}M_{v}q_{0}}\theta_{2}(fN_{w}f)
. This proves the main assertion of part (2).

Since
\theta_{2}(fN_{w}f)\subset q_{0}M_{v}q_{0}
, Remark
2.3
provides a nonzero projection

s\in\theta_{2}(fN_{w}f)^{\prime}\cap q_{0}M_{v}q_{0}
such that the inclusion

\theta_{2}(fN_{w}f)s\subset sM_{v}s
has finite index.
Since
\theta_{2}(fN_{w}f)s
is isomorphic to
tN_{w}t
, for some projection
t\in N_{w}
, this finishes the proof.
∎

11.
From stable isomorphism to isomorphism: Proof of Theorem
D

In this section we complete the proof of Theorem
D
.

Theorem 11.1
.

Let
\Gamma
and
\Lambda
be two finite simple graphs. Assume that
\Gamma
has no isolated vertices, no leaves and girth at least
5
.
Let
(M_{v})_{v\in\Gamma}
,
(N_{w})_{w\in\Lambda}
be families of II
1
factors. Denote by
M_{\Gamma}=*_{v,\Gamma}M_{v}
and
N_{\Lambda}=*_{w,\Lambda}N_{w}
the associated graph product II
1
factors.
Let
\theta:M_{\Gamma}^{t}\rightarrow N_{\Lambda}
be a
*
-isomorphism, for some
t>0
, and let
\alpha:\Gamma\to\Lambda
be a graph isomorphism such that
\theta(M_{v}^{t})\prec^{s}_{N_{\Lambda}}N_{\alpha(v)}
and
N_{\alpha(v)}\prec_{N_{\Lambda}}^{s}\theta(M_{v}^{t})
, for every vertex
v\in\Gamma
.

Then
t=1
and for every
v\in\Gamma
there exists a unitary
u_{v}\in N_{\Lambda}
such that
\theta(M_{v})=u_{v}N_{\alpha(v)}u_{v}^{*}
.

Moreover, if in addition
\Gamma
has no separating star, then there exists a unitary
u\in N_{\Lambda}
such that
\theta(M_{v})=uN_{\alpha(v)}u^{*}
, for every
v\in\Gamma
.

Before proving Theorem
11.1
, we first explain how Theorem
D
from the introduction follows.

Proof of Theorem
D
.

Let
\Gamma,\Lambda
be finite simple graphs of girth at least
5
with no vertices of valence
0
or
1
, and let
M_{\Gamma}
and
N_{\Lambda}
be graph products over
\Gamma,\Lambda
, with II
1
factor vertex algebras. Let
t>0
, and let
\theta:M_{\Gamma}\to N_{\Lambda}^{t}
be an isomorphism. Remark
2.12
ensures that
\Gamma,\Lambda
are transvection-free and strongly reduced. Therefore, Theorem
C
guarantees the existence of a graph isomorphism
\alpha:\Gamma\to\Lambda
such that
\theta(M_{v})\prec^{s}_{N_{\Lambda}^{t}}N_{\alpha(v)}^{t}
and
N_{\alpha(v)}^{t}\prec^{s}_{N_{\Lambda}^{t}}\theta(M_{v})
, for every
v\in\Gamma
.
Let
s=1/t>0
and
\theta_{0}:M_{\Gamma}^{s}\rightarrow N_{\Lambda}
the
*
-isomorphism obtained by amplifying
\theta
. Then we have
\theta_{0}(M_{v}^{s})\prec_{N_{\Lambda}}^{s}N_{\alpha(v)}

and
N_{\alpha(v)}\prec_{N_{\Lambda}}^{s}\theta_{0}(M_{v}^{s})
, for every
v\in\Gamma
.
The conclusion now follows readily from Theorem
11.1
.
∎

The rest of the section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
11.1
.

Proof of Theorem
11.1
.

By symmetry, we may assume that
t\leq 1
. Put
P=M_{\Gamma}
. We identify
P^{t}=pPp
, for a projection
p\in P
with
\tau(p)=t
, and
pPp=N_{\Lambda}
, via
\theta
. For integers
\ell\geq k\geq 1
, we consider the usual non-unital embedding
\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\subset\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})
. We denote by
Tr
the non-normalized trace on
\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})
and by
e_{i,j}\in\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C}),1\leq i,j\leq k,
the elementary matrices, so that
\text{Tr}(e_{i,i})=1
, for every
1\leq i\leq k
.

The proof is divided between several claims. We start with the following claim.

Claim 11.2
.

For every
v\in\Gamma
, we can find integers
k_{v},\ell_{v}\geq 1
with
1\in\{k_{v},\ell_{v}\}
, projections
p_{v}\in\mathbb{M}_{k_{v}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v}
and
q_{v}\in\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{v}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\emph{lk}(v)}
, and a partial isometry
\delta_{v}\in\mathbb{M}_{k_{v}\ell_{v}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes P
such that, identifying

\mathbb{M}_{k_{v}\ell_{v}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\emph{st}(v)}
with
(\mathbb{M}_{k_{v}}\otimes M_{v})\overline{\otimes}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{v}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\emph{lk}(v)})
in the natural way, we have

(11.1)

\begin{array}[]{lr}\delta_{v}\delta_{v}^{*}=e_{1,1}\otimes p,\\
\delta_{v}^{*}\delta_{v}=p_{v}\otimes q_{v},\\
\delta_{v}(p_{v}(\mathbb{M}_{k_{v}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v})p_{v}\otimes q_{v})\delta_{v}^{*}=e_{1,1}\otimes N_{\alpha(v)}.\end{array}

Moreover, we may take
k_{v},\ell_{v}
as the smallest integers with
(\emph{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v})\leq k_{v}
and
(\emph{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v})\leq\ell_{v}
.

Proof of Claim
11.2
.

Let
v\in\Gamma
and
e\in M_{v}
be a projection with
\tau(e)=\tau(p)
.
Since
M_{v}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{\alpha(v)}
and
N_{\alpha(v)}\prec_{P}^{s}M_{v}
, Proposition
3.10
implies that
M_{\text{st}(v)}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))}
and
N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))}\prec_{P}^{s}M_{\text{st}(v)}
. By Lemma
2.16
we get that
M_{\text{st}(v)}^{\prime}\cap P=\mathbb{C}
and
N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))}^{\prime}\cap pPp=\mathbb{C}p
.

We observe that for every proper subgraph
\Lambda^{\prime}\subsetneq\mathrm{st}(\alpha(v))
, we have
M_{\mathrm{st}(v)}\not\prec_{P}N_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
. Otherwise, as a consequence of Lemma
2.16
(1) and our assumption that all vertex algebras are factors,
N_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
is a factor. Proposition
2.4
(3) would then imply that
M_{\mathrm{st}(v)}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
, and Proposition
2.4
(2) would further give that
N_{\mathrm{st}(\alpha(v))}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
, contradicting Lemma
2.16
(2). Likewise, for every proper subgraph
\Gamma^{\prime}\subsetneq\mathrm{st}(v)
, we have
N_{\mathrm{st}(\alpha(v))}\not\prec_{P}M_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
.

Next, we apply Theorem
3.14
. We have
eM_{\mathrm{st}(v)}e\subset ePe
and
N_{\mathrm{st}(\alpha(v))}\subset pPp
. Since
\tau(p)=\tau(e)
and
P
is a II
1
factor, we can find a partial isometry
\delta\in P
such that
\delta\delta^{*}=p
and
\delta^{*}\delta=e
. Thus, we have
\delta eM_{\mathrm{st}(v)}e\delta^{*}\subset pPp=N_{\Lambda}
. By applying Theorem
3.14
to this inclusion we get a unitary
u\in\mathcal{U}(N_{\Lambda})
such that
u(\delta eM_{\mathrm{st}(v)}e\delta^{*})u^{*}\subset N_{\mathrm{st}(\alpha(v))}
. Thus, the partial isometry
\eta=u\delta\in P
satisfies
\eta\eta^{*}=p
,
\eta^{*}\eta=e
, and

\mathrm{Ad}(\eta)(eM_{\text{st}(v)}e)\subset N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))}.

Similarly, we also find a partial isometry
\xi\in P
such that
\xi\xi^{*}=e,\xi^{*}\xi=p
, and

\text{Ad}(\xi)(N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))})\subset eM_{\text{st}(v)}e.

Thus, we derive that

(11.2)

\text{Ad}(\eta\xi)(N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))})\subset\text{Ad}(\eta)(eM_{\text{st}(v)}e)\subset N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))}.

By using (
11.2
), Proposition
3.9
gives that
\eta\xi\in\mathcal{U}(N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))})
. Hence,
\text{Ad}(\eta\xi)(N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))})=N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))}
and
\text{Ad}(\eta)(eM_{\text{st}(v)}e)=N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))}
. Further, since
M_{v}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{\alpha(v)}
and
N_{\alpha(v)}\prec_{P}^{s}M_{v}
, Lemma
2.18
implies that
\text{Ad}(\eta)(eM_{v}e)\prec^{s}_{N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))}}N_{\alpha(v)}
and
N_{\alpha(v)}\prec^{s}_{N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))}}\text{Ad}(\eta)(eM_{v}e)
.

Since
N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))}=N_{\alpha(v)}\overline{\otimes}N_{\text{lk}(\alpha(v))}
and
eM_{\text{st}(v)}e=eM_{v}e\overline{\otimes}M_{\text{lk}(v)}
, Lemma
2.8
provides a unitary
\zeta\in N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))}
and a decomposition
eM_{\text{st}(v)}e=M_{v}^{t_{1}}\overline{\otimes}M_{\text{lk}(v)}^{t_{2}}
, for some
t_{1},t_{2}>0
with
t_{1}t_{2}=t
, such that
\text{Ad}(\zeta\eta)(M_{v}^{t_{1}})=N_{\alpha(v)}
.
Let
k,\ell
be any positive integers such that
k\geq t_{1}
and
\ell\geq t_{2}
. Let
p_{0}\in\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v}
and
q_{0}\in\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v)}
be projections such that
(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{0})=t_{1}
and
(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{0})=t_{2}
. If we identify
(\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v})\overline{\otimes}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v)})=\mathbb{M}_{k\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{st}(v)}
, then
(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{0}\otimes q_{0})=t_{1}t_{2}=t
. Therefore,
M_{\text{st}(v)}^{t}\cong eM_{\text{st}(v)}e
can be identified with
(p_{0}(\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v})p_{0})\overline{\otimes}(q_{0}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v)})q_{0})
in such a way that we have
M_{v}^{t_{1}}=p_{0}(\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v})p_{0}
and
M_{\text{lk}(v)}^{t_{2}}=q_{0}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v)})q_{0}
. Thus, the claim in particular holds for the smallest integers
k_{v},\ell_{v}
such that
k_{v}\geq t_{1}
and
\ell_{v}\geq t_{2}
. Since
t_{1}t_{2}=t\leq 1
, we have
t_{1}\leq 1
or
t_{2}\leq 1
and hence
1\in\{k_{v},\ell_{v}\}
.
∎

From now on we fix
k_{v},\ell_{v},p_{v},q_{v},\delta_{v}
given by Claim
11.2
, for every vertex
v\in\Gamma
. We define

A=\{v\in\Gamma\mid(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v})\leq 1\}
and
B=\{v\in\Gamma\mid(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v})\leq 1\}
.

Since by Claim
11.2
we have

(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v})(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v})=(\mathrm{Tr}\otimes\tau)(\delta_{v}^{*}\delta_{v})=(\mathrm{Tr}\otimes\tau)(\delta_{v}\delta_{v}^{*})=t\leq 1,

it follows that
A\cup B=\Gamma
.
The moreover assertion of Claim
11.2

also gives that
v\in A
if and only if
k_{v}=1
(in which case
p_{v}\in M_{v}
), and that
v\in B
if and only if
\ell_{v}=1
(in which case
q_{v}\in M_{\text{lk}(v)}
).

Claim 11.3
.

If
v\in\Gamma
and
v^{\prime}\in\emph{lk}(v)
, then
(\emph{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v})=(\emph{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v^{\prime}})
and
(\emph{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v})=(\emph{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v^{\prime}})
.
In particular,
v\in A
if and only if
v^{\prime}\in B
, and
(k_{v},\ell_{v})=(\ell_{v^{\prime}},k_{v^{\prime}})
.

Proof of Claim
11.3
.

Let
v\in\Gamma
and
v^{\prime}\in\text{lk}(v)
. Since
M_{\text{st}(v)}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))}
and
M_{\text{st}(v^{\prime})}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v^{\prime}))}
, we get that
M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))}
and
M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v^{\prime}))}
.
By applying Corollary
3.5
we derive that
M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{\text{st}(\alpha(v))\cap\text{st}(\alpha(v^{\prime}))}
.
Since
\Lambda
is triangle-free, we get that
\text{st}(\alpha(v))\cap\text{st}(\alpha(v^{\prime}))=\{\alpha(v),\alpha(v^{\prime})\}
and thus
M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{\{\alpha(v),\alpha(v^{\prime})\}}
. Similarly, we get that
N_{\{\alpha(v),\alpha(v^{\prime})\}}\prec_{P}^{s}M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}
.

We also observe that
M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}\not\prec_{P}N_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
, for every proper full subgraph
\Lambda^{\prime}\subsetneq\{\alpha(v),\alpha(v^{\prime})\}
, and that
N_{\{\alpha(v),\alpha(v^{\prime})\}}\not\prec_{P}M_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
, for every proper full subgraph
\Gamma^{\prime}\subsetneq\{v,v^{\prime}\}
, see the argument in the second paragraph of the proof of Claim
11.2
.

Since
\Gamma
and
\Lambda
are triangle-free, we also derive that
\{v,v^{\prime}\}^{\perp}=\{\alpha(v),\alpha(v^{\prime})\}^{\perp}=\emptyset
. By Lemma
2.16
(1) we get that
M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}^{\prime}\cap P=\mathbb{C}
and
N_{\{\alpha(v),\alpha(v^{\prime})\}}^{\prime}\cap pPp=\mathbb{C}p
. Let
e\in M_{v}
be a projection with
\tau(e)=\tau(p)
.
By reasoning as in the proof of Claim
11.2
we find a partial isometry
\rho\in P
such that
\rho^{*}\rho=e
,
\rho\rho^{*}=p
and
\text{Ad}(\rho)(eM_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}e)=N_{\{\alpha(v),\alpha(v^{\prime})\}}
. Since
M_{v}\prec_{P}^{s}N_{\alpha(v)}
and
N_{\alpha(v)}\prec_{P}^{s}M_{v}
, Lemma
2.18
implies that
\text{Ad}(\rho)(eM_{v}e)\prec^{s}_{N_{\{\alpha(v),\alpha(v^{\prime})\}}}N_{\alpha(v)}
and
N_{\alpha(v)}\prec^{s}_{N_{\{\alpha(v),\alpha(v^{\prime})\}}}\text{Ad}(\rho)(eM_{v}e)
.

Since
M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}=M_{v}\overline{\otimes}M_{v^{\prime}}
and
N_{\{\alpha(v),\alpha(v^{\prime})\}}=N_{\alpha(v)}\overline{\otimes}N_{\alpha(v^{\prime})}
, by applying Lemma
2.8
we can find a unitary
\gamma\in N_{\{\alpha(v),\alpha(v^{\prime})\}}
and a decomposition
eM_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}e=M_{v}^{s_{1}}\overline{\otimes}M_{v^{\prime}}^{s_{2}}
, for some
s_{1},s_{2}>0
with
s_{1}s_{2}=t
, such that
\text{Ad}(\gamma\rho)(M_{v}^{s_{1}})=N_{\alpha(v)}
and
\text{Ad}(\gamma\rho)(M_{v^{\prime}}^{s_{2}})=N_{\alpha(v^{\prime})}
.

Let
k\geq\max\{s_{1},k_{v},k_{v^{\prime}}\}
and
\ell\geq\max\{s_{2},\ell_{v},\ell_{v^{\prime}}\}
be integers, chosen with
k=\ell
. Note that even though
k=\ell
, it will be useful to keep two different letters in order to better distinguish the embeddings of
\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})
in
\mathbb{M}_{k\ell}(\mathbb{C})
through the identification
\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{M}_{k\ell}(\mathbb{C})
. Since
k\geq s_{1}
and
\ell\geq s_{2}
, there are projections
r\in\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v}
,
r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v^{\prime}}
such that
(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(r)=s_{1}
and
(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(r^{\prime})=s_{2}
. By using the previous paragraph and arguing as in the end of the proof of Claim
11.2
we derive the existence of a unitary
w\in\mathbb{M}_{k\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes P
such that

(11.3)

\begin{split}&w(r(\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v})r\otimes r^{\prime})w^{*}=e_{1,1}\otimes N_{\alpha(v)}\;\;\;\text{and}\\
&w(r\otimes r^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v^{\prime}})r^{\prime})w^{*}=e_{1,1}\otimes N_{\alpha(v^{\prime})}.\end{split}

Using that
k\geq k_{v}
and
\ell\geq\ell_{v}
, we consider the non-unital embedding
\mathbb{M}_{k_{v}\ell_{v}}(\mathbb{C})\subset\mathbb{M}_{k\ell}(\mathbb{C})
induced from the non-unital embeddings
\mathbb{M}_{k_{v}}(\mathbb{C})\subset\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{v}}(\mathbb{C})\subset\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})
. Similarly, we consider the embedding
\mathbb{M}_{k_{v^{\prime}}\ell_{v^{\prime}}}(\mathbb{C})\subset\mathbb{M}_{k\ell}(\mathbb{C})
. Altogether, this allows us to view
\delta_{v},\delta_{v^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{M}_{k\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes P
.
Let
\omega=w^{*}\delta_{v},\omega^{\prime}=w^{*}\delta_{v^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{M}_{k\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes P
. Claim
11.2
and Equation (
11.3
) together imply that
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
are partial isometries such that
\omega^{*}\omega=p_{v}\otimes q_{v},\omega\omega^{*}=r\otimes r^{\prime},\omega^{\prime*}\omega^{\prime}=p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes q_{v^{\prime}},\omega^{\prime}\omega^{\prime*}=r\otimes r^{\prime}
,

(11.4)

\omega(p_{v}(\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v})p_{v}\otimes q_{v})\omega^{*}=r(\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v})r\otimes r^{\prime}

and

(11.5)

\omega^{\prime}(p_{v^{\prime}}(\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v^{\prime}})p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes q_{v^{\prime}})\omega^{\prime*}=r\otimes r^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v^{\prime}})r^{\prime}.

By using Proposition
3.9
(1), we get that
\omega\in\mathbb{M}_{k\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{st}(v)}
and
\omega^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{k\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{st}(v^{\prime})}
.
Since
\omega\in\mathbb{M}_{k\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{st}(v)}
, by combining (
11.4
) and Lemma
2.9
, it follows that

(11.6)

(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(r)
and
(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(r^{\prime})
.

We next claim that

(11.7)

\text{($\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v^{\prime}})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(r^{\prime})$\;\;\; and \;\;\; $(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v^{\prime}})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(r)$}.

Since
k=\ell
, there is
y\in\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{M}_{k\ell}(\mathbb{C}))
such that
\text{Ad}(y)(\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes 1)=1\otimes\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})
.
By taking relative commutants, we also have
\text{Ad}(y)(1\otimes\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C}))=\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes 1
. Put
z=y\otimes 1\in\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{M}_{k\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes P)
. Since
r\in\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v}
and
r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v^{\prime}}
, we get that
s=\text{Ad}(z)(r)\in\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v}
and
s^{\prime}=\text{Ad}(z)(r^{\prime})\in\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v^{\prime}}
.
Moreover,
\text{Ad}(z)(r\otimes r^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v^{\prime}})r^{\prime})=s\otimes s^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v^{\prime}})s^{\prime}
, which in combination with (
11.5
) gives that

(z\omega^{\prime})(p_{v^{\prime}}(\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v^{\prime}})p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes q_{v^{\prime}})(z\omega^{\prime})^{*}=s\otimes s^{\prime}(\mathbb{M}_{k}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v^{\prime}})s^{\prime}.

Since
z\omega^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{k\ell}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{st}(v^{\prime})}
, we can apply Lemma
2.9
to conclude that

\text{$(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v^{\prime}})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(s^{\prime})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(r^{\prime})$\;\;\; and \;\;\; $(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v^{\prime}})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(s)=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(r)$}.

This proves (
11.7
) which together with (
11.6
) implies the claim. ∎

Our next goal is to prove that for adjacent vertices
v,v^{\prime}\in\Gamma
, the partial isometry
\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v}
admits a special decomposition given by the following claim.

Claim 11.4
.

Let
v\in\Gamma
,
v^{\prime}\in\emph{lk}(v)
, and let
m\geq\max\{k_{v},\ell_{v},k_{v^{\prime}},\ell_{v^{\prime}}\}
be an integer.
Then there exist partial isometries
\alpha_{v^{\prime},v},\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes P
such that

(1)

\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v}=\alpha_{v^{\prime},v}^{*}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}
.

(2)

\alpha_{v^{\prime},v}\in 1\otimes M_{\emph{lk}(v^{\prime})}
and
\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\emph{lk}(v)}
if
v\in A
.

(3)

\alpha_{v^{\prime},v}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\emph{lk}(v^{\prime})}
and
\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\in 1\otimes M_{\emph{lk}(v)}
if
v\in B
.

(4)

\alpha_{v^{\prime},v}\alpha_{v^{\prime},v}^{*}=p_{v}
and
\alpha_{v^{\prime},v}^{*}\alpha_{v^{\prime},v}=q_{v^{\prime}}
.

(5)

\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}^{*}=p_{v^{\prime}}
and
\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}^{*}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}=q_{v}
.

As
1\in\{k_{v},\ell_{v}\}
and
1\in\{k_{v^{\prime}},\ell_{v^{\prime}}\}
, we have
m\geq\max\{k_{v}\ell_{v},k_{v^{\prime}}\ell_{v^{\prime}}\}
. This allows in Claim (
11.4
) to view
\delta_{v},\delta_{v^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes P
. Also, Claim (
11.2
) gives that
\delta_{v}\delta_{v}^{*}=e_{1,1}\otimes p=\delta_{v^{\prime}}\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}
and that
\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes P
is a partial isometry with left and right supports
p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes q_{v^{\prime}}
and
p_{v}\otimes q_{v}
, respectively.

Proof of Claim
11.4
.

Let
v\in\Gamma
and
v^{\prime}\in\text{lk}(v)
.
If
v\in B
, then
v^{\prime}\in A
by Claim
11.3
.
Thus, in order to prove the claim, it suffices to show that if
v\in A
, then there are partial isometries
\alpha_{v^{\prime},v},\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes P
satisfying (1), (2), (4) and (5).

Since
v\in A
, Claim
11.3
gives that
v^{\prime}\in B
. Hence
k_{v}=\ell_{v^{\prime}}=1
. Since
m\geq\ell_{v}
and
m\geq k_{v^{\prime}}
, by Claim
11.2
we can view
\delta_{v},\delta_{v^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes P
. Moreover, recall that
\delta_{v}^{*}\delta_{v}=p_{v}\otimes q_{v}
and
\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v^{\prime}}=p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes q_{v^{\prime}}
, where
p_{v}\in M_{v},q_{v}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v)},p_{v^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v^{\prime}}
,
q_{v^{\prime}}\in M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}
and we identify
\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{st}(v)}=M_{v}\overline{\otimes}(\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v)})
and
\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{st}(v^{\prime})}=(\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v^{\prime}})\overline{\otimes}M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}
.

Denote

Q=p_{v}M_{v}p_{v}\otimes q_{v}
,
R=p_{v^{\prime}}(\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v^{\prime}})p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes q_{v^{\prime}}
and
S=p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes q_{v^{\prime}}M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}q_{v^{\prime}}
. By using Claim
11.2
we deduce that

(11.8)

\begin{split}[\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v})(Q),R]&=\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*})([\text{Ad}(\delta_{v})(Q),\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}})(R)])\\
&=\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*})\big([e_{1,1}\otimes N_{\alpha(v)},e_{1,1}\otimes N_{\alpha(v^{\prime})}]\big)=\{0\}.\end{split}

Since
R^{\prime}\cap(p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes q_{v^{\prime}})(\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes P)(p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes q_{v^{\prime}})=S
by Lemma
2.16
(1), using (
11.8
) we derive that

(11.9)

\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v})(Q)\subset S.

Write
\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v})(Q)=p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes T
, for a von Neumann subalgebra
T\subset q_{v^{\prime}}M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}q_{v^{\prime}}
.
We claim that

(11.10)

T\prec_{M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}}M_{v}
and
T^{\prime}\cap q_{v^{\prime}}M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}q_{v^{\prime}}=\mathbb{C}q_{v^{\prime}}
.

Assume by contradiction that
T\nprec_{M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}}M_{v}
. Then
Theorem
2.1
provides a net
(u_{i})\subset\mathcal{U}(T)
such that
\|\text{E}_{M_{v}}(a^{*}u_{i}b)\|_{2}\rightarrow 0
, for every
a,b\in M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}
. The proof of Lemma
2.18
implies that
\|\text{E}_{M_{v}}(a^{*}u_{i}b)\|_{2}\rightarrow 0
, for every
a,b\in P
.
Recalling that
Q=p_{v}M_{v}p_{v}\otimes q_{v}
, it follows that

\|\text{E}_{Q}(\text{Ad}(\delta_{v}^{*}\delta_{v^{\prime}})(p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes u_{i}))\|_{2}\rightarrow 0
.
This contradicts the fact that
\text{Ad}(\delta_{v}^{*}\delta_{v^{\prime}})(p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes u_{i})\in Q
and
\|\text{Ad}(\delta_{v}^{*}\delta_{v^{\prime}})(p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes u_{i})\|_{2}=\|p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes u_{i}\|_{2}=\|p_{v^{\prime}}\|_{2}>0
, for every
i
, proving the first assertion of (
11.10
).

To prove the second assertion from (
11.10
), note that Claim
11.2
gives that
\text{Ad}(\delta_{v})(Q)=e_{1,1}\otimes N_{\alpha(v)}
and
\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}})(R)=e_{1,1}\otimes N_{\alpha(v^{\prime})}
. By using Lemma
2.16
(1) to take relative commutants in the latter equality, we get that
\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}})(S)=e_{1,1}\otimes N_{\text{lk}(\alpha(v^{\prime}))}
.
Since
\text{lk}(\alpha(v))\cap\text{lk}(\alpha(v^{\prime}))=\emptyset
, Lemma
2.16
(1) further implies that
N_{\alpha(v)}^{\prime}\cap N_{\text{lk}(\alpha(v^{\prime}))}=\mathbb{C}p
.
By combining the last two facts we get that

(11.11)

\begin{split}\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v})(Q)^{\prime}\cap S&=\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*})(\text{Ad}(\delta_{v})(Q)^{\prime}\cap\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}})(S))\\
&=\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*})(e_{1,1}\otimes(N_{\alpha(v)}^{\prime}\cap N_{\text{lk}(\alpha(v^{\prime}))}))\\
&=\mathbb{C}\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*})(e_{1,1}\otimes p)=\mathbb{C}(p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes q_{v^{\prime}}).\end{split}

Since
\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v})(Q)^{\prime}\cap S=p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes(T^{\prime}\cap q_{v^{\prime}}M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}q_{v^{\prime}})
, it follows that
T^{\prime}\cap q_{v^{\prime}}M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}q_{v^{\prime}}=\mathbb{C}q_{v^{\prime}}
, which finishes the proof of (
11.10
).

Since
M_{v}
is a factor,
Q
and
T
are also factors. By using (
11.10
) and that
\text{lk}(v)\cap\text{lk}(v^{\prime})=\emptyset
, Theorem
3.14
implies the existence of a partial isometry
\lambda\in M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}
such that
\lambda^{*}\lambda=q_{v^{\prime}}
and
\text{Ad}(\lambda)(T)\subset M_{v}
. Since
\tau(q_{v^{\prime}})=\tau(p_{v})
and
M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}
is a factor, we may assume that
\lambda\lambda^{*}=p_{v}
and hence
\text{Ad}(\lambda)(T)\subset p_{v}M_{v}p_{v}
. Denote
z=(p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes\lambda)\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes P
. Then

z
is a partial isometry such that
zz^{*}=p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes p_{v},z^{*}z=p_{v}\otimes q_{v}
, and using that
\text{Ad}(\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v})(Q)=p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes T
implies that

(11.12)

\text{Ad}(z)(Q)=\text{Ad}(p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes\lambda)(p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes T)=p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes\text{Ad}(\lambda)(T)\subset p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes p_{v}M_{v}p_{v}.

Since
Q=p_{v}M_{v}p_{v}\otimes q_{v}
, combining (
11.12
) and Proposition
3.9
(1) implies that
z\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C}){\otimes}M_{\text{st}(v)}
. Moreover, note that
\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C}){\otimes}M_{\text{st}(v)}=(\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v)})\overline{\otimes}M_{v}
,
p_{v}\in M_{v}
,
q_{v},p_{v^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v)}
,
zz^{*}=p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes p_{v}
and
z^{*}z=q_{v}\otimes p_{v}
. By applying Lemma
2.9
we find partial isometries
\sigma\in M_{v}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v)}
such that
\sigma\sigma^{*}=\sigma^{*}\sigma=p_{v}
,
\beta\beta^{*}=p_{v^{\prime}},\beta^{*}\beta=q_{v}
and
z=\beta\otimes\sigma
.

Since
z=(p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes\lambda)\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v}
, we get that

\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v}=(p_{v^{\prime}}\otimes\lambda^{*})(\beta\otimes\sigma)=(1\otimes\lambda^{*})(p_{v^{\prime}}\beta\otimes 1)(1\otimes\sigma)=(1\otimes\lambda^{*}\sigma)(\beta\otimes 1).

Since
\lambda\in M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}
,
\sigma\in M_{v}
,
\lambda^{*}\lambda=q_{v^{\prime}}
and
\lambda\lambda^{*}=\sigma\sigma^{*}=\sigma^{*}\sigma=p_{v}
, we get that

\alpha=\lambda^{*}\sigma\in M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}
is a partial isometry with
\alpha\alpha^{*}=q_{v^{\prime}}
and
\alpha^{*}\alpha=p_{v}
. This finishes the proof of Claim
11.4
.
∎

We are ready to prove that
t=1
. We will achieve this by analyzing cycles of minimal length in
\Gamma
.

Claim 11.5
.

We have
t=1
.

Moreover, let
\Gamma_{0}\subset\Gamma
be a connected component of
\Gamma
and

v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n-1},v_{n}=v_{0}
be a cycle of minimal length in
\Gamma_{0}
.
Then
(\emph{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v_{i}})=(\emph{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v_{i}})=1
, thus
k_{v_{i}}=\ell_{v_{i}}=1
and
p_{v_{i}}=q_{v_{i}}=1
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
. Additionally,
\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i-1}}\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}^{*}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{i-1}})\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{i+1}})
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
.

Proof of Claim
11.5
.

We will prove the moreover assertion, and along the way show that
t=1
.
Put
m=\max\{k_{v_{i}},\ell_{v_{i}}\mid 0\leq i\leq n-1\}
.
By Claim
11.3
,
v_{0}\in B
or
v_{1}\in B
. Thus, after possibly relabeling the cycle, we may assume that
v_{0}\in B
.
We treat two cases, depending on whether
n
is even or odd.
The case when
n
is even is the more challenging of the two cases because, unlike in the case when
n
is odd, we cannot easily show that
k_{v_{i}}
and
\ell_{v_{i}}
are equal to
1
. As such, when
n
is even, we will need to carefully keep track of various matrix amplifications.

Case 1.

n
is even.

Since
v_{0}\in B
, Claim
11.3
implies by induction that
v_{i}\in B
, if
i
is even, and
v_{i}\in A
, if
i
is odd.
We identify
\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{st}(v_{i})}
with
(\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v_{i}})\overline{\otimes}M_{\text{lk}(v_{i})}
, if
i
is even, and
M_{v_{i}}\overline{\otimes}(\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v_{i})})
, if
i
is odd.
We use these identifications to view
\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v_{i}}
and
M_{\text{lk}(v_{i})}
, if
i
is even, and respectively,
M_{v_{i}}
and
\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v_{i})}
, if
i
is odd, as unital subalgebras of
\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes P
.
These identifications give rise to unital embeddings of the form
Q\otimes R\subset\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes P
. In order to avoid confusion over which tensor product is being used we will write
xy
instead of
x\otimes y
, for
x\in Q
and
y\in R
.

By applying Claim
11.4
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
, we find partial isometries
\alpha_{i-1,i}:=\alpha_{v_{i-1},v_{i}}
and
\alpha_{i,i-1}:=\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i-1}}

(with indices considered modulo
n
, i.e.,
-1
is identified to
n-1
) such that the following conditions hold:

(1)

\delta_{v_{i-1}}^{*}\delta_{v_{i}}=\alpha_{{i-1},i}^{*}\alpha_{i,{i-1}}
.

(2)

\alpha_{i-1,i}\in M_{\text{lk}(v_{i-1})}
and
\alpha_{i,i-1}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v_{i})}
, if
i
is odd.

(3)

\alpha_{{i-1},i}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v_{i-1})}
and
\alpha_{i,{i-1}}\in M_{\text{lk}(v_{i})}
, if
i
is even.

(4)

\alpha_{{i-1},i}\alpha_{{i-1},i}^{*}=p_{v_{i}}
and
\alpha_{{i-1},i}^{*}\alpha_{{i-1},i}=q_{v_{i-1}}
.

(5)

\alpha_{i,{i-1}}\alpha_{i,{i-1}}^{*}=p_{v_{i-1}}
and
\alpha_{i,{i-1}}^{*}\alpha_{i,{i-1}}=q_{v_{i}}
.

With our convention, we have
\alpha_{n,i}=\alpha_{0,i}
and
\alpha_{i,n}=\alpha_{i,0}
, whenever
i\in\{1,n-1\}
.

Further, by combining (1) with (
11.1
), we get that

(11.13)

\begin{split}\alpha_{0,1}^{*}\alpha_{1,0}\alpha_{1,2}^{*}\alpha_{2,1}\cdots\alpha_{{n-1},0}^{*}\alpha_{0,{n-1}}&=(\delta_{v_{0}}^{*}\delta_{v_{1}})(\delta_{v_{1}}^{*}\delta_{v_{2}})\dots(\delta_{v_{n-1}}^{*}\delta_{v_{n}})\\
&=\delta_{v_{0}}^{*}(\delta_{v_{1}}\delta_{v_{1}}^{*})\dots(\delta_{v_{n-1}}\delta_{v_{n-1}}^{*})\delta_{v_{n}}\\
&=\delta_{v_{0}}^{*}\delta_{v_{0}}=p_{v_{0}}q_{v_{0}}.\end{split}

By combining (4), (5) and (
11.13
) we get that

(11.14)

\begin{split}&\alpha_{0,1}(p_{v_{0}}q_{v_{0}})\alpha_{0,{n-1}}^{*}\\
&=(\alpha_{0,1}\alpha_{0,1}^{*})(\alpha_{1,0}\cdots\alpha_{{n-1},0}^{*})(\alpha_{0,{n-1}}\alpha_{0,{n-1}}^{*})\\
&=p_{v_{1}}(\alpha_{1,0}\alpha_{1,2}^{*}\alpha_{2,1}\cdots\alpha_{{n-2},{n-1}}^{*}\alpha_{{n-1},{n-2}}\alpha_{{n-1},0}^{*})p_{v_{n-1}}\\
&=(p_{v_{1}}\alpha_{1,0}\alpha_{1,2}^{*}\alpha_{2,1})(\alpha_{2,3}^{*}\alpha_{3,2}\cdots\alpha_{{n-3},{n-2}}^{*}\alpha_{{n-2},{n-3}})(\alpha_{{n-2},{n-1}}^{*}\alpha_{{n-1},{n-2}}\alpha_{{n-1},n}^{*}p_{v_{n-1}})\end{split}

Since
\alpha_{0,1}\in M_{\text{lk}(v_{0})}q_{v_{0}}
and
\alpha_{0,n-1}^{*}\in q_{v_{0}}M_{\text{lk}(v_{0})}

n
(as
n
is even) by (2)-(5), and
p_{v_{0}}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v_{0}}
, we get
\alpha_{0,1}(p_{v_{0}}q_{v_{0}})\alpha_{0,n-1}^{*}=p_{v_{0}}(\alpha_{0,1}q_{v_{0}}\alpha_{0,n-1}^{*})=p_{v_{0}}(\alpha_{0,1}\alpha_{0,n-1}^{*})
.
Since
p_{v_{1}}\in M_{v_{1}},\alpha_{1,0},\alpha_{1,2}^{*}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v_{1})}
and
\alpha_{2,1}\in p_{v_{1}}M_{\text{lk}(v_{2})}
, we also get
p_{v_{1}}\alpha_{1,0}\alpha_{1,2}^{*}\alpha_{2,1}=\alpha_{1,0}\alpha_{1,2}^{*}p_{v_{1}}\alpha_{2,1}=\alpha_{1,0}\alpha_{1,2}^{*}\alpha_{2,1}
. Similarly, we deduce that
\alpha_{{n-2},{n-1}}^{*}\alpha_{{n-1},{n-2}}\alpha_{{n-1},n}^{*}p_{v_{n-1}}=\alpha_{{n-2},{n-1}}^{*}\alpha_{{n-1},{n-2}}\alpha_{{n-1},n}^{*}
. By combining these three facts, (
11.14
) rewrites as

(11.15)

p_{v_{0}}(\alpha_{0,1}\alpha_{0,n-1}^{*})=\alpha_{1,0}\alpha_{1,2}^{*}\alpha_{2,1}\alpha_{2,3}^{*}\alpha_{3,2}\cdots\alpha_{{n-3},{n-2}}^{*}\alpha_{{n-2},{n-3}}\alpha_{{n-2},{n-1}}^{*}\alpha_{{n-1},{n-2}}\alpha_{{n-1},n}^{*}.

Next, we claim that

(11.16)

\mathcal{W}_{\text{lk}(v_{0})}\cap\left(\mathcal{W}_{\text{lk}(v_{1})}\cdots\mathcal{W}_{\text{lk}(v_{n-1})}\right)\subset\mathcal{W}_{v_{1}}\mathcal{W}_{v_{n-1}}.

To justify (
11.16
), we note that since the cycle
\{v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n-1},v_{n}=v_{0}\}
has minimal length in its connected component, and
\Gamma
has no leaves, no isolated vertices and girth at least
5
, we get that
n\geq 5
and
\text{lk}(v_{0})\cap(\cup_{i=1}^{n-1}\text{lk}(v_{i}))=\{v_{1},v_{n-1}\}
. From this we deduce that

(11.17)

\mathcal{W}_{\text{lk}(v_{0})}\cap\left(\mathcal{W}_{\text{lk}(v_{1})}\cdots\mathcal{W}_{\text{lk}(v_{n-1})}\right)\subset\mathcal{W}_{\text{lk}(v_{0})}\cap\mathcal{W}_{\cup_{i=1}^{n-1}\text{lk}(v_{i})}=\mathcal{W}_{\text{lk}(v_{0})\cap(\cup_{i=1}^{n-1}\text{lk}(v_{i}))}=\mathcal{W}_{\{v_{1},v_{n-1}\}},

where the first equality is a classical fact about intersections of parabolic subgroups in Coxeter groups
[
Ti74
,
So76
]
. Let
g\in\mathcal{W}_{\{v_{1},v_{n-1}\}}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{v_{1}}\mathcal{W}_{v_{n-1}}
. Then
v_{n-1}
appears at least once before
v_{1}
in the reduced form
g=g_{1}\cdots g_{d}
of
g
, i.e., there exist
1\leq i<j\leq d
such that
g_{i}=v_{n-1}
and
g_{j}=v_{1}
. On the other hand, we have that
v_{1}\in\text{lk}(v_{2})
,
v_{1}\not\in\text{lk}(v_{b})
, for every
b\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}\setminus\{2\}
,
v_{n-1}\in\text{lk}(v_{n-2})
, and
v_{n-1}\not\in\text{lk}(v_{c})
, for every
c\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}\setminus\{n-2\}
. This implies that
g\not\in\mathcal{W}_{\text{lk}(v_{1})}\cdots\mathcal{W}_{\text{lk}(v_{n-1})}
. Together with (
11.17
), we get
\mathcal{W}_{\text{lk}(v_{0})}\cap\left(\mathcal{W}_{\text{lk}(v_{1})}\cdots\mathcal{W}_{\text{lk}(v_{n-1})}\right)\subset\mathcal{W}_{v_{1}}\mathcal{W}_{v_{n-1}}
, which proves (
11.16
)

By combining (
11.16
) with Lemma
2.15
, we get that

\text{E}_{M_{\text{lk}(v_{0})}}(M_{\text{lk}(v_{1})}\cdots M_{\text{lk}(v_{n-1})})\subset\overline{\text{sp}}(\{\mathring{M}_{\bf v}\mid v\in\mathcal{W}_{v_{1}}\mathcal{W}_{v_{n-1}}\})\subset\overline{\text{sp}}(M_{v_{1}}M_{v_{n-1}}).

Since
\alpha_{i,i-1}\alpha_{i,i+1}^{*}
belongs to
M_{\text{lk}(v_{i})}
or
\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{\text{lk}(v_{i})}
, for every
1\leq i\leq n-1
, we deduce that

(11.18)

\text{E}_{M_{\text{lk}(v_{0})}}(\alpha_{1,0}\alpha_{1,2}^{*}\alpha_{2,1}\cdots\alpha_{{n-2},{n-1}}^{*}\alpha_{{n-1},{n-2}}\alpha_{{n-1},n}^{*})\in\overline{\text{sp}}(M_{v_{1}}M_{v_{n-1}})
.

On other hand, since
p_{v_{0}}\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\otimes M_{v_{0}}
and
\alpha_{0,1}\alpha_{0,n-1}^{*}\in M_{\text{lk}(v_{0})}
, we get that

(11.19)

\text{E}_{M_{\text{lk}(v_{0})}}(p_{v_{0}}(\alpha_{0,1}\alpha_{0,n-1}^{*}))=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v_{0}})\alpha_{0,1}\alpha_{0,n-1}^{*}.

By combining (
11.15
), (
11.18
) and (
11.19
), we conclude that
\zeta:=\alpha_{0,1}\alpha_{0,n-1}^{*}
belongs to
\overline{\text{sp}}(M_{v_{1}}M_{v_{n-1}})
. Moreover,
\zeta
is a partial isometry such that
\zeta\zeta^{*}=p_{v_{1}}\in M_{v_{1}}
and
\zeta^{*}\zeta=p_{v_{n-1}}\in M_{v_{n-1}}
. Since
\Gamma
is triangle-free,
v_{1}
and
v_{n-1}
are not adjacent, hence
M_{\{v_{1},v_{n-1}\}}=M_{v_{1}}*M_{v_{n-1}}
. Applying Lemma
2.10
we deduce that
\zeta
is a unitary,
\zeta\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{1}})\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{n-1}})
, thus
\alpha_{0,n-1}\alpha_{0,1}^{*}=\zeta^{*}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{n-1}})\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{1}})
.

Since
\zeta
is a unitary,
p_{v_{1}}=1
. As
v_{1}\in A
, we have
(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v_{1}})\leq(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v_{1}})
. Since
p_{v_{1}}=1
and
(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v_{1}})(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v_{1}})=t\leq 1
, we conclude that
t=1
and
(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v_{1}})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v_{1}})=1
. By using Claim
11.3
and induction, we get
(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v_{i}})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v_{i}})=1
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
. Thus,
k_{v_{i}}=\ell_{v_{i}}=1
and
p_{v_{i}}=q_{v_{i}}=1
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
. In particular,
v_{i}\in B
and repeating the argument above gives that
\alpha_{i,{i-1}}\alpha_{i,{i+1}}^{*}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{i-1}})\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{i+1}})
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
. This proves the claim in the case
n
is even.

Case 2.

n
is odd.

Since
n
is odd, Claim
11.3
and induction imply that
(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v_{0}})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v_{n-1}})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v_{0}})
.
Thus,

(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v_{0}})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v_{0}})=\sqrt{t}
. Similarly, we get
(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v_{i}})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v_{i}})=\sqrt{t}\leq 1
. Therefore,
k_{v_{i}}=\ell_{v_{i}}=1
and
v_{i}\in A\cap B
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
.
By Claim
11.4
for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
we can find partial isometries
\alpha_{i-1,i}:=\alpha_{v_{i-1},v_{i}}\in M_{\text{lk}(v_{i-1})}
and
\alpha_{i,i-1}:=\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i-1}}\in M_{\text{lk}(v_{i})}
such that (1) and (4)-(5) hold. The conclusion now follows by repeating the argument used in Case 1.
∎

Next, we put together the main findings of this proof so far in the following claim.

Claim 11.6
.

For every
v\in\Gamma
, we have
k_{v}=\ell_{v}=1
and
p_{v}=q_{v}=1
. Thus,
\delta_{v}\in P
is a unitary such that
\delta_{v}M_{v}\delta_{v}^{*}=N_{\alpha(v)}
. Moreover, for every
v\in\Gamma
and
v^{\prime}\in\emph{lk}(v)
,
\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v}=\alpha_{v^{\prime},v}^{*}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}
, for unitaries
\alpha_{v^{\prime},v}\in M_{\emph{lk}(v^{\prime})}
and
\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\in M_{\emph{lk}(v)}
.

Proof of Claim
11.6
.

Let
\Gamma_{0}
be the connected component of
v
in
\Gamma
. Let
v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n-1},v_{n}=v_{0}
be a cycle of minimal length in
\Gamma_{0}
. Claim
11.5
gives that
(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v_{0}})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v_{0}})=1
. We have
v_{0}\in\Gamma_{0}
,
\Gamma_{0}
is connected, and by Claim
11.3
the set
\{w\in\Gamma\mid(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{w})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{w})=1\}
has the property that if
w
belong to this set, then any neighbour of
w
belongs to the set. We thus get
(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(p_{v})=(\text{Tr}\otimes\tau)(q_{v})=1
. Claims
11.2
and
11.4
now give the conclusion.
∎

Claims
11.5
and
11.6
imply the main assertion of Theorem
11.1
. To prove the moreover assertion, assume additionally that
\Gamma
has no separating star (in particular,
\Gamma
is connected). By Claim
11.6
,
\delta_{v}M_{v}\delta_{v}^{*}=N_{\alpha(v)}
, for every
v\in\Gamma
. The moreover assertion amounts to showing that we can take the unitary
\delta_{v}\in P
to be independent of
v
.
We will first show that this is the case along minimal cycles, see Claim
11.8
.

Towards this goal, we fix for the rest of the proof a cycle

v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n-1},v_{n}=v_{0}
of minimal length in
\Gamma
.
By applying Claim
11.5
, we have
\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i-1}}\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}^{*}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{i-1}})\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{i+1}})
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
.
Hence, there exist
\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i-1},v_{i+1}}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{i-1}})
and
\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i+1},v_{i-1}}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{i+1}})
such that

(11.20)

\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i-1}}\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}^{*}=\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i-1},v_{i+1}}\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i+1},v_{i-1}}^{*}
,     for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
.

Claim 11.7
.

\alpha_{v_{i-1},v_{i},v_{i-2}}^{*}\alpha_{v_{i+1},v_{i},v_{i+2}}\in\mathbb{C}1
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
.

Proof of Claim
11.7
.

Using that
\delta_{v_{i}}^{*}\delta_{v_{i+1}}=\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}^{*}\alpha_{v_{i+1},v_{i}}
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
, we get that

1=(\delta_{v_{0}}^{*}\delta_{v_{1}})(\delta_{v_{1}}^{*}\delta_{v_{2}})\cdots(\delta_{v_{n-1}}^{*}\delta_{v_{n}})=(\alpha_{v_{0},v_{1}}^{*}\alpha_{v_{1},v_{0}})(\alpha_{v_{1},v_{2}}^{*}\alpha_{v_{2},v_{1}})\cdots(\alpha_{v_{n-1},v_{0}}^{*}\alpha_{v_{0},v_{n-1}}),

and thus

(11.21)

(\alpha_{v_{0},v_{n-1}}\alpha_{v_{0},v_{1}}^{*})(\alpha_{v_{1},v_{0}}\alpha_{v_{1},v_{2}}^{*})\cdots(\alpha_{v_{n-1},v_{n-2}}\alpha_{v_{n-1},v_{0}}^{*})=1.

By combining (
11.20
) and (
11.21
) we further get that

(\alpha_{v_{0},v_{n-1},v_{1}}\alpha_{v_{0},v_{1},v_{n-1}}^{*})(\alpha_{v_{1},v_{0},v_{2}}\alpha_{v_{1},v_{2},v_{0}}^{*})\cdots(\alpha_{v_{n-1},v_{n-2},v_{0}}\alpha_{v_{n-1},v_{0},v_{n-2}}^{*})=1.

Since
\alpha_{v_{n-1},v_{0},v_{n-2}}\in M_{v_{0}}
and
\alpha_{v_{0},v_{n-1},v_{1}}\in M_{v_{n-1}}
commute, by applying
\text{Ad}(\alpha_{v_{n-1},v_{0},v_{n-2}}^{*}\alpha_{v_{0},v_{n-1},v_{1}}^{*})
to the last identity we derive that

\alpha_{v_{n-1},v_{0},v_{n-2}}^{*}\alpha_{v_{0},v_{1},v_{n-1}}^{*}(\alpha_{v_{1},v_{0},v_{2}}\alpha_{v_{1},v_{2},v_{0}}^{*})\cdots(\alpha_{v_{n-2},v_{n-3},v_{n-1}}\alpha_{v_{n-2},v_{n-1},v_{n-3}}^{*})\alpha_{v_{n-1},v_{n-2},v_{0}}\alpha_{v_{0},v_{n-1},v_{1}}=1.

Since
\alpha_{v,v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}}\in M_{v^{\prime}}
, and
M_{v}
commutes with
M_{v^{\prime}}
whenever
v,v^{\prime}\in\Gamma
are adjacent, the last displayed identity rewrites as

(11.22)

(\alpha_{v_{n-1},v_{0},v_{n-2}}^{*}\alpha_{v_{1},v_{0},v_{2}})(\alpha_{v_{0},v_{1},v_{n-1}}^{*}\alpha_{v_{2},v_{1},v_{3}})\cdots(\alpha_{v_{n-2},v_{n-1},v_{n-3}}^{*}\alpha_{v_{0},v_{n-1},v_{1}})=1.

Thus, if we denote
\rho_{i}=\alpha_{v_{i-1},v_{i},v_{i-2}}^{*}\alpha_{v_{i+1},v_{i},v_{i+2}}
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
, then
\rho_{0}\rho_{1}\cdots\rho_{n-1}=1
. Since
\rho_{i}\in M_{v_{i}}
, we deduce that
\rho_{i}\in\mathbb{C}1
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
, which proves the claim.
∎

Next, we prove the following claim.

Claim 11.8
.

There exists a unitary
\mu\in P
such that
\mu M_{v_{i}}\mu^{*}=N_{\alpha(v_{i})}
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
.

Proof of Claim
11.8
.

For
0\leq i\leq n-1
, put
\beta_{i}:=\alpha_{v_{i-1},v_{i},v_{i-2}}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{i}})
. Then Claim
11.7
gives

(11.23)

\mathbb{C}\beta_{i}=\mathbb{C}\alpha_{v_{i-1},v_{i},v_{i-2}}=\mathbb{C}\alpha_{v_{i+1},v_{i},v_{i+2}}
,    for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
.

Combining (
11.20
) and (
11.23
) gives that

\mathbb{C}\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i-1}}\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}^{*}=\mathbb{C}\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i-1},v_{i+1}}\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i+1},v_{i-1}}^{*}=\mathbb{C}\beta_{i-1}\beta_{i+1}^{*}
and therefore

(11.24)

\mathbb{C}\beta_{i-1}^{*}\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i-1}}=\mathbb{C}\beta_{i+1}^{*}\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}
,     for every
0\leq i\leq n-1.

For
0\leq i\leq n-1
, put
\gamma_{i}=\beta_{i-1}^{*}\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i-1}}
. Since
\beta_{i-1}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{i-1}})
and
\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i-1}}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{\text{lk}(v_{i})})
, we get that
\gamma_{i}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{\text{lk}(v_{i})})
. Moreover, using the definition of
\gamma_{i}
for the first equality, and Equation (
11.24
) for the second, we get

(11.25)

\mathbb{C}\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i-1}}=\mathbb{C}\beta_{i-1}\gamma_{i}
and
\mathbb{C}\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}=\mathbb{C}\beta_{i+1}\gamma_{i}
,     for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
.

By using Claim
11.4
,
and that
\beta_{i+1}
and
\beta_{i}
commute we get that for
0\leq i\leq n-1
we have

(11.26)

\begin{split}\mathbb{C}\delta_{v_{i}}^{*}\delta_{v_{i+1}}=\mathbb{C}\alpha_{v_{i},v_{i+1}}^{*}\alpha_{v_{i+1},v_{i}}&=\mathbb{C}(\beta_{i+1}\gamma_{i})^{*}(\beta_{i}\gamma_{i+1})\\
&=\mathbb{C}(\gamma_{i}^{*}\beta_{i+1}^{*}\beta_{i}\gamma_{i+1})=\mathbb{C}((\gamma_{i}^{*}\beta_{i})(\beta_{i+1}^{*}\gamma_{i+1}))\\
&=\mathbb{C}((\beta_{i}^{*}\gamma_{i})^{*}(\beta_{i+1}^{*}\gamma_{i+1})).\end{split}

Hence,
\mathbb{C}\delta_{v_{i}}(\beta_{i}^{*}\gamma_{i})^{*}=\mathbb{C}\delta_{v_{i+1}}(\beta_{i+1}^{*}\gamma_{i+1})^{*}
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
. Let
\mu=\delta_{v_{0}}(\beta_{0}^{*}\gamma_{0})^{*}\in\mathcal{U}(P)
. Then for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
, we have
\mathbb{C}\delta_{v_{i}}(\beta_{i}^{*}\gamma_{i})^{*}=\mathbb{C}\mu
and hence
\delta_{v_{i}}\in\mathbb{C}\mu\beta_{i}^{*}\gamma_{i}
. Since
\beta_{i}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v_{i}})
and
\gamma_{i}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{\text{lk}(v_{i})})
, by Claim
11.2
we deduce that

N_{\alpha(v_{i})}=\text{Ad}(\delta_{v_{i}})(M_{v_{i}})=\text{Ad}(\mu\beta_{i}^{*}\gamma_{i})(M_{v_{i}})=\text{Ad}(\mu)(M_{v_{i}})
,   for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
.

This finishes the proof of the claim.
∎

In order to finish the proof, it will suffice to prove the following claim.

Claim 11.9
.

There exists a unitary
u\in P
such that
uM_{v}u^{*}=N_{\alpha(v)}
, for every
v\in\Gamma
.

Proof.

Since
\mu M_{v_{i}}\mu^{*}=N_{\alpha(v_{i})}
by Claim
11.8
, after replacing
\theta
by
\text{Ad}(\mu)\circ\theta
, we have
M_{v_{i}}=N_{\alpha(v_{i})}
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
. Thus,
we may assume from now on that the unitaries
(\delta_{v})_{v\in\Gamma}\subset P
provided by Claim
11.2
satisfy
\delta_{v_{i}}=1
, for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
, in addition to satisfying Claim
11.6
.

We next use an idea from the proof of
[
Se89
, Theorem 6]
to show that

(11.27)

\text{$\delta_{v}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{\text{st}(v)})$,\;\;\; for every $v\in\Gamma$}.

Let
v\in\Gamma
and
v^{\prime}\in\Gamma\setminus\text{st}(v)
.
We claim that there exists
0\leq i\leq n-1
such that
v_{i}\not\in\text{st}(v^{\prime})
. Otherwise, if all
v_{i}
belong to
\mathrm{st}(v^{\prime})
, then since
n\geq 4
, we would find
0\leq j<n-1
such that
v_{j},v_{j+1}\in\text{lk}(v^{\prime})
. Then
v^{\prime},v_{j}
and
v_{j+1}
form a triangle, a contradiction.

Since
v_{i},v\in\Gamma\setminus\text{st}(v^{\prime})
and
\Gamma
contains no separating stars,
v_{i}
and
v
are connected in
\Gamma\setminus\text{st}(v^{\prime})
. Let
w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{k}
be a path in
\Gamma\setminus\text{st}(v^{\prime})
such that
w_{0}=v_{i}
and
w_{k}=v
.
Then
\delta_{w_{0}}=1
,
\delta_{w_{k}}=\delta_{v}
, thus

(11.28)

\begin{split}\delta_{v}=\delta_{w_{0}}^{*}\delta_{w_{k}}&=(\delta_{w_{0}}^{*}\delta_{w_{1}})\cdots(\delta_{w_{k-1}}^{*}\delta_{w_{k}})\\
&=(\alpha_{w_{0},w_{1}}^{*}\alpha_{w_{1},w_{0}})(\alpha_{w_{1},w_{2}}^{*}\alpha_{w_{2},w_{1}})\cdots(\alpha_{w_{k-1},w_{k}}^{*}\alpha_{w_{k},w_{k-1}})\\
&=\alpha_{w_{0},w_{1}}^{*}(\alpha_{w_{1},w_{0}}\alpha_{w_{1},w_{2}}^{*})\cdots(\alpha_{w_{k-1},w_{k-2}}\alpha_{w_{k-1},w_{k}}^{*})\alpha_{w_{k},w_{k-1}}\end{split}

Since
\alpha_{w,w^{\prime}}\in M_{\text{lk}(w)}
, for every
w,w^{\prime}\in\Gamma
with
w^{\prime}\in\text{lk}(w)
, we get that
\delta_{v}\in M_{\text{lk}(w_{0})}M_{\text{lk}(w_{1})}\cdots M_{\text{lk}(w_{k})}
. Hence,
\delta_{v}\in M_{\cup_{j=0}^{k}\text{lk}(w_{j})}
. Since
w_{j}\not\in\text{st}(v^{\prime})
, we get that
v^{\prime}\not\in\text{lk}(w_{j})
, for every
0\leq j\leq k
. Thus,
\cup_{j=0}^{k}\text{lk}(w_{j})\subset\Gamma\setminus\{v^{\prime}\}
and
\delta_{v}\in M_{\Gamma\setminus\{v^{\prime}\}}
. Since this holds for every
v^{\prime}\in\Gamma\setminus\text{st}(v)
, we deduce that

\delta_{v}\in\bigcap_{v^{\prime}\in\Gamma\setminus\text{st}(v)}M_{\Gamma\setminus\{v^{\prime}\}}=M_{\text{st}(v)},

which proves (
11.27
).

We continue by proving that

(11.29)

\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime\prime}}^{*}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v^{\prime}})\mathcal{U}(M_{v^{\prime\prime}}),
for every
v\in\Gamma
and
v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\in\text{lk}(v)
.

Let
v\in\Gamma
and
v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\in\text{lk}(v)
. By Claim
11.4
we get
\alpha_{v^{\prime},v}^{*}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}=\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v}
, thus
\delta_{v}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}^{*}=\delta_{v^{\prime}}\alpha_{v^{\prime},v}^{*}
.
On the other hand, since
\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\in M_{\text{lk}(v)}
and
\alpha_{v^{\prime},v}\in M_{\text{lk}(v^{\prime})}
, by using (
11.27
) we get
\delta_{v}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}^{*}\in M_{\text{st}(v)}
and
\delta_{v^{\prime}}\alpha_{v^{\prime},v}^{*}\in M_{\text{st}(v^{\prime})}
. Since
\Gamma
contains no triangles, we also have
\text{st}(v)\cap\text{st}(v^{\prime})=\{v,v^{\prime}\}
, hence
M_{\text{st}(v)}\cap M_{\text{st}(v^{\prime})}=M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}.
Altogether, we deduce that

z^{\prime}:=\delta_{v}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}^{*}\in M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}
.
Similarly, we have

z^{\prime\prime}:=\delta_{v}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime\prime}}^{*}\in M_{\{v,v^{\prime\prime}\}}
.
These facts together give that

\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime\prime}}^{*}=z^{\prime*}z^{\prime\prime}\in M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}M_{\{v,v^{\prime\prime}\}}\subset M_{\{v,v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\}}.

Since
\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime\prime}}^{*}\in M_{\text{lk}(v)}
and
\{v,v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\}\cap\text{lk}(v)=\{v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\}
, we conclude that

\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime\prime}}^{*}=z^{\prime*}z^{\prime\prime}\in M_{\{v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\}}.

Hence, the unitaries
z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime}\in M_{\{v,v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\}}=(M_{v^{\prime}}*M_{v^{\prime\prime}})\overline{\otimes}M_{v}
satisfy
z^{\prime}\in M_{v^{\prime}}\overline{\otimes}M_{v},z^{\prime\prime}\in M_{v^{\prime\prime}}\overline{\otimes}M_{v}
and
z^{\prime*}z^{\prime\prime}\in(M_{v^{\prime}}*M_{v^{\prime\prime}})\overline{\otimes}\mathbb{C}1
. We can thus apply Corollary
2.11
to derive that
z^{\prime*}z^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v^{\prime}})\mathcal{U}(M_{v^{\prime\prime}})
and thus
\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime\prime}}^{*}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v^{\prime}})\mathcal{U}(M_{v^{\prime\prime}})
. This finishes the proof of (
11.29
).

Next, (
11.29
) implies that for every
v\in\Gamma
and
v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\in\text{lk}(v)
, there exist
\alpha_{v,v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v^{\prime}})
and
\alpha_{v,v^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v^{\prime\prime}})
such that

(11.30)

\text{$\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime\prime}}^{*}=\alpha_{v,v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime}}^{*}$, for every $v\in\Gamma$ and $v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{lk}(v)$}.

Fix
v\in\Gamma
.
We claim that

(11.31)

\alpha_{v^{\prime},v,w^{\prime}}^{*}\alpha_{v^{\prime\prime},v,w^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathbb{C}1
, for every
v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\in\text{lk}(v),w^{\prime}\in\text{lk}(v^{\prime})\setminus\{v\}
and
w^{\prime\prime}\in\text{lk}(v^{\prime\prime})\setminus\{v\}
.

Since
\Gamma
is triangle-free we have that
w^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime}\not\in\text{st}(v)
. Since
\Gamma
has no separating star,
\Gamma\setminus\text{st}(v)
is connected and therefore
w^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime\prime}
are connected by a path in
\Gamma\setminus\text{st}(v)
. In particular, there is a sequence

t_{0},t_{1},\cdots,t_{l-1},t_{l}=t_{0}
in
\Gamma
in which any two consecutive terms are adjacent such that
t_{l-2}=w^{\prime},t_{l-1}=v^{\prime},t_{0}=v,t_{1}=v^{\prime\prime}
,
t_{2}=w^{\prime\prime}
and
t_{i}\not=v
, for every
1\leq i\leq k-1
. By (
11.30
) we have that

\alpha_{t_{i},t_{i-1}}\alpha_{t_{i},t_{i+1}}^{*}=\alpha_{t_{i},t_{i-1},t_{i+1}}\alpha_{t_{i},t_{i+1},t_{i-1}}^{*}
, for every
0\leq i\leq l-1
. By repeating the calculation from the proof of Claim
11.7
we get that

(11.32)

(\alpha_{t_{l-1},t_{0},t_{l-2}}^{*}\alpha_{t_{1},t_{0},t_{2}})(\alpha_{t_{0},t_{1},t_{l-1}}^{*}\alpha_{t_{2},t_{1},t_{3}})\cdots(\alpha_{t_{l-2},t_{l-1},t_{l-3}}^{*}\alpha_{t_{0},t_{l-1},t_{1}})=1.

Thus, if we denote
\zeta_{i}=\alpha_{t_{i-1},t_{i},t_{i-2}}^{*}\alpha_{t_{i+1},t_{i},t_{i+2}}
, for every
0\leq i\leq l-1
, then
\zeta_{0}\zeta_{1}\cdots\zeta_{l-1}=1
.
Since
\zeta_{i}\in M_{t_{i}}
, for every
0\leq i\leq l-1
and
t_{0}\not\in\{t_{1},\cdots,t_{l-1}\}
, we deduce that
\zeta_{0}\in\mathbb{C}1
. Hence, we have
\alpha_{v^{\prime},v,w^{\prime}}^{*}\alpha_{v^{\prime\prime},v,w^{\prime\prime}}=\alpha_{t_{l-1},t_{0},t_{l-2}}^{*}\alpha_{t_{1},t_{0},t_{2}}=\zeta_{0}\in\mathbb{C}1
,
which proves (
11.31
).

Next, (
11.31
) implies that for every
v\in\Gamma
, we can find
\alpha_{v}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v})
such that

(11.33)

\alpha_{v^{\prime},v,w^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{C}\alpha_{v}
, for every
v^{\prime}\in\text{lk}(v)
and
w^{\prime}\in\text{lk}(v^{\prime})\setminus\{v\}
.

If
v\in\Gamma
, then combining (
11.33
) and (
11.30
) gives that for every
v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\in\text{lk}(v)
with
v^{\prime}\not=v^{\prime\prime}
we have

\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime\prime}}^{*}=\alpha_{v,v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime}}^{*}\in\mathbb{C}\alpha_{v^{\prime}}\alpha_{v^{\prime\prime}}^{*}
,

hence
\mathbb{C}\alpha_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}=\mathbb{C}\alpha_{v^{\prime\prime}}^{*}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime\prime}}
. Let
\sigma_{v}:=\alpha_{v^{\prime\prime}}^{*}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime\prime}}
, for some
v^{\prime\prime}\in\text{lk}(v)
.
Then
\mathbb{C}\alpha_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}=\mathbb{C}\sigma_{v}
, thus

(11.34)

\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{C}\alpha_{v^{\prime}}\sigma_{v}
, for every
v\in\Gamma
and
v^{\prime}\in\text{lk}(v)
.

Moreover, since
\alpha_{v^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{v^{\prime\prime}})\subset\mathcal{U}(M_{\text{lk}(v)})
and
\alpha_{v,v^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{\text{lk}(v)})
, we get that
\sigma_{v}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{\text{lk}(v)})
.

By combining Claim
11.4
and (
11.34
), we deduce that for any two adjacent vertices
v,v^{\prime}\in\Gamma
we have

(11.35)

\begin{split}\mathbb{C}\delta_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\delta_{v}=\mathbb{C}\alpha_{v^{\prime},v}^{*}\alpha_{v,v^{\prime}}&=\mathbb{C}(\alpha_{v}\sigma_{v^{\prime}})^{*}(\alpha_{v^{\prime}}\sigma_{v})\\
&=\mathbb{C}(\sigma_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\alpha_{v}^{*}\alpha_{v^{\prime}}\sigma_{v})=\mathbb{C}(\sigma_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\alpha_{v^{\prime}}\alpha_{v}^{*}\sigma_{v})\\
&=\mathbb{C}((\alpha_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\sigma_{v^{\prime}})^{*}(\alpha_{v}^{*}\sigma_{v})).\end{split}

Therefore,
\mathbb{C}\delta_{v}(\alpha_{v}^{*}\sigma_{v})^{*}=\mathbb{C}\delta_{v^{\prime}}(\alpha_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\sigma_{v^{\prime}})^{*}
, for every adjacent vertices
v,v^{\prime}\in\Gamma
. Since
\Gamma
is connected, we conclude that

\mathbb{C}\delta_{v}(\alpha_{v}^{*}\sigma_{v})^{*}=\mathbb{C}\delta_{v^{\prime}}(\alpha_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\sigma_{v^{\prime}})^{*}
, for every
v,v^{\prime}\in\Gamma
. Put
u=\delta_{v^{\prime}}(\alpha_{v^{\prime}}^{*}\sigma_{v^{\prime}})\in\mathcal{U}(P)
, for some
v^{\prime}\in\Gamma
. Then
\mathbb{C}\delta_{v}(\alpha_{v}^{*}\sigma_{v})^{*}=\mathbb{C}u
and thus
\delta_{v}\in\mathbb{C}u\alpha_{v}^{*}\sigma_{v}
, for every
v\in\Gamma
.

Finally, since
\alpha_{v}\in M_{v}
and
\sigma_{v}\in\mathcal{U}(M_{\text{lk}(v)})
, using Claim
11.2
we deduce that

N_{\alpha(v)}=\text{Ad}(\delta_{v})(M_{v})=\text{Ad}(u\alpha_{v}^{*}\sigma_{v})(M_{v})=\text{Ad}(u)(M_{v}),

for every
v\in\Gamma
.
This finishes the proof of the claim and of the theorem.
∎

12.
Applications: classification results and calculations of symmetries

In this section, we use our main results to derive Corollaries
E
-
H
stated in the introduction.

12.1.
W^{*}
-classification of right-angled Artin groups

As a consequence of Theorem
B
, we derive the following statement.

Corollary
E
.

Let
\Gamma
and
\Lambda
be two finite simple graphs which are transvection-free.

Then the right-angled Artin groups
A_{\Gamma}
and
A_{\Lambda}
are W
∗
-equivalent if and only if they are isomorphic.

Proof.

Clearly if
A_{\Gamma}
and
A_{\Lambda}
are isomorphic, then they are W
∗
-equivalent. Conversely, if
A_{\Gamma}
and
A_{\Lambda}
are W
∗
-equivalent, then
\text{L}(A_{\Gamma})\cong\text{L}(A_{\Lambda})
.
Hence, if we put
\mathcal{A}=\text{L}(\mathbb{Z})
, then

*_{v,\Gamma}\mathcal{A}\cong\text{L}(A_{\Gamma})\cong\text{L}(A_{\Lambda})\cong*_{w,\Lambda}\mathcal{A}.

Since
\mathcal{A}
is a diffuse abelian (thus, amenable) tracial von Neumann algebra, Theorem
B
implies that the graphs
\Gamma
and
\Lambda
are isomorphic.
∎

The results established in Section
8
also allow to cover some right-angled Artin groups beyond the transvection-free case. Here is an example.

Example 12.1
.

If we only assume that
\Gamma,\Lambda
and their untransvectable subgraphs
\Gamma^{u}
and
\Lambda^{u}
are clique-reduced, and
\text{L}(A_{\Gamma})\cong\text{L}(A_{\Lambda})
, then Theorem
8.1
still ensures that
\mathcal{E}(\Gamma^{u})
and
\mathcal{E}(\Lambda^{u})
are isomorphic. This provides classification results for right-angled Artin groups beyond the transvection-free case. For instance, for
n\geq 1
, if
P_{n}
denotes the path with
n
edges and
n+1
vertices, then
P_{n}^{u}
is the path with
n-1
vertices (the two leaves are the only transvectable vertices). Noticing that
\mathcal{E}(P_{n}^{u})\cong P_{n}^{u}
, it follows that for
m,n\geq 1
we have
\text{L}(A_{P_{m}})\cong\text{L}(A_{P_{n}})
if and only if
m=n
. We mention that the measure equivalence (or orbit equivalence) classification of these right-angled Artin groups is still open. We also mention that for
m\neq n
,
A_{P_{m}}
and
A_{P_{n}}
are not commensurable unless
\{m,n\}=\{3,4\}
by
[
CRKZ21
]
, and all right-angled Artin groups
A_{P_{n}}
with
n\geq 3
are quasi-isometric by
[
BN08
]
).

12.2.
Classification of graph products of amenable II
1
factors

Our next corollary follows from our main theorem in the case of II
1
factor vertex algebras, Theorem
8.1
. The following statement provides a generalization of Corollary
F
from the introduction (Corollary
F
specializes Corollary
12.2
to the case where the graphs
\Gamma
and
\Lambda
are transvection-free).

Corollary 12.2
.

Let
\Gamma
and
\Lambda
be two
clique-reduced finite simple graphs. For every
v\in\Gamma
and
w\in\Lambda
, let
M_{v}=N_{w}=R
, where
R
denotes the hyperfinite II
1
factor.

If
M_{\Gamma}
and
N_{\Lambda}
are isomorphic, then the untransvectable subgraphs
\Gamma^{u}
and
\Lambda^{u}
are isomorphic.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Theorem
8.1
(2).
∎

Remark 12.3
.

Our assumption that the graphs are clique-reduced is mild: if
\Gamma
contains a collapsible clique
C
, then by collapsing
C
to a vertex, we get a new graph product structure for
M_{\Gamma}
, where the new vertex algebra is
\overline{\otimes}_{v\in C}R\cong R
, where
R
is the hyperfinite II
1
factor.

Remark 12.4
.

Corollary
12.2
recovers
[
CC25
, Theorem 4.10]
in the case when
\Gamma
and
\Lambda
are finite. The latter result asserts that if
\Gamma
,
\Lambda
are H-rigid and
M_{\Gamma}\cong N_{\Lambda}
, then
\text{Int}(\Gamma)\cong\text{Int}(\Lambda)
.
Recall from
[
CC25
, Definition 4.1]
that a nonempty set
\Gamma_{0}\subset\Gamma
is called an internal set
if
\Gamma_{0}^{\perp}
is not a clique; a vertex
v\in\Gamma
is called an internal vertex if
\{v\}
is an internal set. When all the internal sets of
\Gamma
are vertices, the internal graph
\mathrm{Int}(\Gamma)
is the full subgraph of
\Gamma
whose vertices are the internal vertices of
\Gamma
. By
[
CC25
, Definition 4.6]
,
\Gamma
is called H-rigid if all of its internal sets are vertices, and every connected component of every full subgraph
\Gamma_{0}\subset\Gamma
with
\Gamma_{0}^{\perp}\not=\emptyset
is a clique.

To justify the above claim, we first observe that we do not lose any generality by assuming that
\Gamma
and
\Lambda
are clique-reduced. Indeed, if
\Gamma
is H-rigid and contains a collapsible clique
C
on at least two vertices, then the definition of H-rigid graphs ensures that
C^{\perp}
is a clique. In particular the link in
\Gamma
of every vertex
w
of
C
is a clique, so
w
is not internal, and not untransvectable. In addition, if
\bar{\Gamma}
is the graph obtained by collapsing
C
to a vertex
v
, then
v
is neither internal nor untransevctable. It follows that
\mathrm{Int}(\Gamma)=\mathrm{Int}(\bar{\Gamma})
and
\Gamma^{u}=\bar{\Gamma}^{u}
.

To recover
[
CC25
, Theorem 4.10]
, it is thus enough to prove that if
\Gamma
is H-rigid and not reduced to a vertex, then the internal graph
\mathrm{Int}(\Gamma)
coincides with our untransvectable subgraph
\Gamma^{u}
. To prove this, note if
v\in\Gamma
is untransvectable, then
\text{lk}(v)
is not a clique, which means that
v\in\text{Int}(\Gamma)
. Conversely, if
v
is transvectable, say
\mathrm{lk}(v)\subset\mathrm{st}(w)
for some vertex
w\in\Gamma\setminus\{v\}
, then the definition of H-rigid graphs implies that
\{v,w\}^{\perp}
is a clique. Since
\mathrm{lk}(v)
is equal to either
\{v,w\}^{\perp}
(if
v
and
w
are non-adjacent) or to
\{w\}\cup\{v,w\}^{\perp}
(if
v
and
w
are adjacent) it follows that
\mathrm{lk}(v)
is a complete graph, so
v\notin\mathrm{Int}(\Gamma)
.

Corollary
12.2
applies to new classes of graphs, not covered by
[
CC25
, Theorem 4.10]
. Indeed, the latter result excludes the existence of full subgraphs
\Gamma_{0}\subset\Gamma
such that
|\Gamma_{0}|\geq 2
and
\Gamma_{0}^{\perp}
is not a clique, which Corollary
12.2
allows.

12.3.
W^{*}
-classification of graph products of ICC groups

Corollary
G
.

Let
\Gamma,\Lambda
be two finite simple graphs of girth at least
5
, which contain no vertices of valence
0
or
1
. Let
(G_{v})_{v\in\Gamma}
and
(H_{w})_{w\in\Lambda}
be families of ICC groups. Let
G_{\Gamma}=*_{v,\Gamma}G_{v}
and
H_{\Lambda}=*_{w,\Lambda}H_{w}
be the associated graph product groups.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

G_{\Gamma}
and
H_{\Lambda}
are W
∗
-equivalent.

(2)

G_{\Gamma}
and
H_{\Lambda}
are stably W
∗
-equivalent.

(3)

There exists a graph isomorphism
\alpha:\Gamma\rightarrow\Lambda
such that
G_{v}
is W
∗
-equivalent to
H_{\alpha(v)}
, for every
v\in\Gamma
.

Proof.

We clearly have (1)
\Rightarrow
(2) and (3)
\Rightarrow
(1). To prove (2)
\Rightarrow
(3), assume that

G_{\Gamma}
and
H_{\Lambda}
are stably W
∗
-equivalent, i.e., there is a
*
-isomorphism
\theta:\text{L}(G_{\Gamma})\rightarrow\text{L}(H_{\Lambda})^{t}
, for some
t>0
. Since
\text{L}(G_{\Gamma})=*_{v,\Gamma}\text{L}(G_{v})
,
\text{L}(H_{\Lambda})=*_{w,\Lambda}\text{L}(H_{w})
, and
\text{L}(G_{v}),\text{L}(H_{w})
are II
1
factors, for every
v\in\Gamma,w\in\Lambda
, Theorem
D
implies that
t=1
and there exist a graph isomorphism
\alpha:\Gamma\rightarrow\Lambda
and for every
v\in\Gamma
a unitary
u_{v}\in\text{L}(H_{\Lambda})
such that
\theta(\text{L}(G_{v}))=u_{v}\text{L}(H_{\alpha(v)})u_{v}^{*}
. Hence,
G_{v}
is W
∗
-equivalent to
H_{\alpha(v)}
, for every
v\in\Gamma
, which proves that (3) holds.
∎

12.4.
Computations of symmetry groups

Corollary
H
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite simple graph of girth at least
5
, which contains no vertices of valence
0
or
1
. Let
(M_{v})_{v\in\Gamma}
be a family of II
1
factors.
Then
\mathcal{F}(M_{\Gamma})=\{1\}
.

Moreover, if
\Gamma
has no separating stars, then

\mathrm{Out}(M_{\Gamma})\cong\left(\bigoplus_{v\in\Gamma}\mathrm{Aut}(M_{v})\right)\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma;M_{\Gamma}).

The reader is referred to the introduction, in particular Equation (
1.1
), for the semi-direct product structure.

Proof.

Applying Theorem
D
to
N_{\Lambda}=M_{\Gamma}
implies that
\mathcal{F}(M_{\Gamma})=\{1\}
.

Assume additionally that
\Gamma
has no separating stars. Then the moreover assertion of Theorem
D
implies that
\text{Aut}(M_{\Gamma})=\text{Aut}_{\text{loc}}(M_{\Gamma})\text{Inn}(M_{\Gamma})
. Hence we have

(12.1)

\text{Out}(M_{\Gamma})=\text{Aut}_{\text{loc}}(M_{\Gamma})\text{Inn}(M_{\Gamma})/\text{Inn}(M_{\Gamma})\cong\text{Aut}_{\text{loc}}(M_{\Gamma})/(\text{Inn}(M_{\Gamma})\cap\text{Aut}_{\text{loc}}(M_{\Gamma})).

We claim that
\text{Inn}(M_{\Gamma})\cap\text{Aut}_{\text{loc}}(M_{\Gamma})=\{\text{Id}\}
.
Let
\theta\in\text{Inn}(M_{\Gamma})\cap\text{Aut}_{\text{loc}}(M_{\Gamma})
. Then
\theta=\text{Ad}(u)
, for some
u\in\mathcal{U}(M_{\Gamma})
and there exists
\sigma\in\text{Aut}(\Gamma)
such that
\theta(M_{v})=M_{\sigma(v)}
, for every
v\in\Gamma
. If
v\in\Gamma
, then
M_{\sigma(v)}=\theta(M_{v})=uM_{v}u^{*}
and Lemma
2.16
(2) implies that
\sigma(v)=v
. Hence,
uM_{v}u^{*}=M_{v}
, and therefore
u\in M_{\text{st}(v)}
, by Proposition
3.9
(1). In conclusion
u\in\cap_{v\in\Gamma}M_{\text{st}(v)}=M_{\cap_{v\in\Gamma}\text{st}(v)}
.
Let us show that
\cap_{v\in\Gamma}\text{st}(v)=\emptyset
. Indeed, if
w\in\cap_{v\in\Gamma}\text{st}(v)
, then
\Gamma=\text{st}(w)
.
Let
w^{\prime}\in\text{lk}(w)
and
w^{\prime\prime}\in\text{lk}(w^{\prime})\setminus\{w\}
. Since
w^{\prime\prime}\in\text{st}(w)
, we get that
w^{\prime\prime}\in\text{lk}(w)
, and thus
\{w,w^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime}\}
forms a triangle, which is a contradiction. Hence, we have shown that
\cap_{v\in\Gamma}\text{st}(v)=\emptyset
and therefore
u\in\mathbb{C}1
. This shows that
\theta=\text{Ad}(u)=\text{Id}
, which finishes the proof of the claim.

The claim, (
12.1
) and (
1.1
) give that
\text{Out}(M_{\Gamma})\cong\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M_{\Gamma})\cong(\bigoplus_{v\in\Gamma}\mathrm{Aut}(M_{v}))\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma;M_{\Gamma})
.
∎

13.
Obstructions to rigidity results

Theorems
A
-
D
provide classes of graph product II
1
factors
M_{\Gamma}
which satisfy the following rigidity statement: for any automorphism
\theta:M_{\Gamma}\rightarrow M_{\Gamma}
, there exists an automorphism
\alpha
of
\Gamma
such that
\theta(M_{v})\prec_{M_{\Gamma}}^{s}M_{\alpha(v)}
, for every
v\in\Gamma
. In all of these results, the graph
\Gamma
is assumed to be transvection-free. The purpose of this section is to show that, conversely, the presence of transvectable vertices is in general an obstruction to such a rigidity statement. We start with the following result.

Proposition 13.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite simple graph,
(M_{v},\tau_{v})_{v\in\Gamma}
a family of tracial von Neumann algebras, and
M_{\Gamma}=*_{v,\Gamma}M_{v}
the associated graph product von Neumann algebra.
Assume that
v,v^{\prime}\in\Gamma
are such that
\emph{lk}(v)\subset\emph{st}(v^{\prime})
and the following condition holds (
\star
) there is
\rho\in\emph{Aut}(M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}})
satisfying
\rho(M_{v})\nprec_{M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}}M_{v}
and
{\rho}(M_{v^{\prime}})=M_{v^{\prime}}
.

Then there exists
\theta\in\emph{Aut}(M_{\Gamma})
such that
\theta(M_{v})\nprec_{M_{\Gamma}}M_{v^{\prime\prime}}
, for every
v^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma
.

Proof.

We first show the existence of an automorphism
\theta
of
M_{\Gamma}
with
\theta_{|M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}}=\rho
and
\theta_{|M_{w}}=\text{Id}_{M_{w}}
, for every
w\in\Gamma\setminus\{v,v^{\prime}\}
. Let
n=|\Gamma|
and enumerate
\Gamma=\{v_{1}=v,v_{2}=v^{\prime},\dots,v_{n}\}
.
For
k\geq 2
, let
\Gamma_{k}
be the full subgraph of
\Gamma
with vertex set
\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}\}
. We claim that for every
2\leq k\leq n
, there is an automorphism
\theta_{k}
of
M_{\Gamma_{k}}

such that
{\theta_{k}}_{|M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}}=\rho
and
{\theta_{k}}_{|M_{w}}=\text{Id}_{M_{w}}
, for every
w\in\Gamma_{k}\setminus\{v,v^{\prime}\}
. Once this claim is proven,
\theta:=\theta_{n}
is the desired automorphism of
M_{\Gamma}
.

We prove the claim by induction on
k
. For
k=2
, the claim holds for
\theta_{2}=\rho
.
Assume that we have constructed
\theta_{k}\in\text{Aut}(M_{\Gamma_{k}})
, for some
2\leq k<n
.
In order to construct
\theta_{k+1}\in\text{Aut}(M_{\Gamma_{k+1}})
, note that
M_{\Gamma_{k+1}}=M_{\Gamma_{k}}*_{B_{k}}(B_{k}\overline{\otimes}M_{v_{k+1}})
, where
B_{k}=M_{\Gamma_{k}\cap\text{lk}(v_{k+1})}
.

If
v\not\in\text{lk}(v_{k+1})
, then

\Gamma_{k}\cap\text{lk}(v_{k+1})\subset\Gamma_{k}\setminus\{v\}
.
Since
\theta_{k}(M_{w})=M_{w}
, for every
w\in\Gamma_{k}\setminus\{v\}
, and
B_{k}
is generated by
\{M_{w}\mid w\in\Gamma_{k}\cap\text{lk}(v_{k+1})\}
, we get that

{\theta_{k}}(B_{k})=B_{k}
. If
v\in\text{lk}(v_{k+1})
, then since
\text{lk}(v)\subset\text{st}(v^{\prime})
and
v^{\prime}\not=v_{k+1}
, we get that
v^{\prime}\in\text{lk}(v_{k+1})
.
Thus,
B_{k}
is generated by
M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}
and
\{M_{w}\mid w\in(\Gamma_{k}\cap\text{lk}(v_{k+1}))\setminus\{v,v^{\prime}\}\}
, which again gives that
\theta_{k}(B_{k})=B_{k}
.
In either case,
\theta_{k}(B_{k})=B_{k}
. This implies that there is
\theta_{k+1}\in\text{Aut}(M_{\Gamma_{k+1}})
such that
{\theta_{k+1}}_{|M_{\Gamma_{k}}}=\theta_{k}
and
{\theta_{k+1}}_{|M_{v_{k+1}}}=\text{Id}_{M_{v_{k+1}}}
, which proves the induction step and the claim.

We finish the proof by showing that
\theta
has the desired property. Assume by contradiction that
\rho(M_{v})=\theta(M_{v})\prec_{M_{\Gamma}}M_{v^{\prime\prime}}
, for some
v^{\prime\prime}\in\Gamma
. Since
M_{v}
is diffuse and
\rho(M_{v})\subset M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}
, applying Corollary
3.5
implies that
v^{\prime\prime}\in\{v,v^{\prime}\}
. Using again that
\rho(M_{v})\subset M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}
and Lemma
2.18
, we derive that
\rho(M_{v})\prec_{M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}}M_{v^{\prime\prime}}
. If
v^{\prime\prime}=v
, this contradicts our assumption that
\rho(M_{v})\nprec_{M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}}M_{v}
. If
v^{\prime\prime}=v^{\prime}
, then
\rho(M_{v})\prec_{M_{v,v^{\prime}}}M_{v^{\prime}}=\rho(M_{v^{\prime}})
, hence
M_{v}\prec_{M_{v,v^{\prime}}}M_{v^{\prime}}
. By Lemma
2.16
(2), this again gives a contradiction.
∎

Remark 13.2
.

In order to apply Proposition
13.1
we need to exhibit examples of pairs of vertices
v,v^{\prime}\in\Gamma
with
\text{lk}(v)\subset\text{st}(v^{\prime})
which satisfy condition
(\star)
. Before proving two general results in this direction (Proposition
13.3
and Corollary
13.4
), let us point out that
(\star)
holds if
M_{v}=M_{v^{\prime}}=\text{L}(\mathbb{Z})
. In this case, we have
M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}=\text{L}(A)
, where
A
is a group generated by elements
v,v^{\prime}
such that
M_{v}=\text{L}(\langle v\rangle)
and
M_{v^{\prime}}=\text{L}(\langle v^{\prime}\rangle)
. Moreover, we have
A\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, if
v^{\prime}\in\text{lk}(v)
, and
A\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}
, if
v^{\prime}\not\in\text{lk}(v)
. Thus, we can define
\rho_{0}\in\text{Aut}(A)
by letting
\rho(v)=vv^{\prime}
and
\rho_{0}(v^{\prime})=v^{\prime}
. Then the automorphism
\rho
of
M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}=\text{L}(A)
associated to
\rho_{0}
(i.e., given by
\rho(u_{g})=u_{\rho_{0}(g)}
, for every
g\in A
) satisfies
(\star)
.

If
v
and
v^{\prime}
are adjacent, the above construction works more generally if
M_{v}=\text{L}(G_{v})
,
M_{v^{\prime}}=\text{L}(G_{v^{\prime}})
and there is a homomorphism with infinite image
\theta:G_{v}\to Z(G_{v^{\prime}})
. In this case, the map which sends
g\in G_{v}
to
g\theta(g)
and is the identity on
G_{v^{\prime}}
, gives an automorphism of
G_{v}\times G_{v^{\prime}}
and thus of
M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}=\text{L}(G_{v}\times G_{v^{\prime}})
which satisfies
(\star)
.

Assume now that
v
and
v^{\prime}
are not adjacent, and that
M_{v}=\text{L}(G_{v})
and
M_{v^{\prime}}=\text{L}(G_{v^{\prime}})
, where
G_{v}
splits as a free product
A\ast B
, and
G_{v^{\prime}}
contains an infinite order element,
h
. Then the map which is identity on
A
and on
G_{v^{\prime}}
, and is the conjugation by
h
on
B
gives an automorphism of
G_{v}\ast G_{v^{\prime}}
and thus of
M_{\{v,v^{\prime}\}}=\text{L}(G_{v}*G_{v^{\prime}})
which satisfies
(\star)
.

In this section, we will extend these constructions to von Neumann algebras that do not necessarily come from groups.

Proposition 13.3
.

Let
(M_{1},\tau_{1})
and
(M_{2},\tau_{2})
be tracial von Neumann algebras. Then the following hold:

(1)

Assume that there exists a
*
-homomorphism
\sigma:M_{1}\rightarrow M_{1}\overline{\otimes}\mathcal{Z}(M_{2})
such that

(a)

\emph{E}_{\mathcal{Z}(M_{2})}(\sigma(x))=\tau(x)1
, for every
x\in M_{1}
,

(b)

\sigma(M_{1})\nprec_{M_{1}\overline{\otimes}\mathcal{Z}(M_{2})}M_{1}
, and

(c)

\sigma(M_{1})
and
\mathcal{Z}(M_{2})
generate
M_{1}\overline{\otimes}\mathcal{Z}(M_{2})
.

Then there exists
\rho\in\emph{Aut}(M_{1}\overline{\otimes}M_{2})
such that
\rho(M_{1})\nprec_{M_{1}\overline{\otimes}M_{2}}M_{1}
and
\rho(M_{2})=M_{2}
.

(2)
